j_{\epsilon}(u^{*}c,x\circ(u\times\mathrm{I})^{\prime},y\circ(u\times\mathrm{I})):\mathrm{I}^{m}\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrow X\,,

and for this map we require that

j_{\epsilon}(u^{*}c,x\circ(u\times\mathrm{I})^{\prime},y\circ(u\times\mathrm{I}))=j_{\epsilon}(c,x,y)\circ(u\times\mathrm{I}).

(35)

This can be seen to be a reformulation of the logical specification given in
[
CCHM18
]
(see
[
AGH21
]
).

Definition 23
.

A
uniform, biased fibration structure
on a map
f:Y\rightarrow X
is a choice of fillers
j_{\epsilon}(c,x,y)
as in (
33
) satisfying (
35
) for all maps
u:\mathrm{I}^{m}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{n}
.

Finally, we have the analogue of proposition
15
for fibrant objects. The analogous statement of proposition
17
for fibrations is omitted, as is the entirely analogous proof.

Corollary 24
.

For any object
X
in
\mathsf{cSet}
the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is
biased fibrant
, in the sense that every map
D\rightarrow X
from the domain of a generating biased trivial cofibration
D\rightarrowtail Z\times\mathrm{I}
extends to a total map
Z\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrow X
,

\mathcal{C}\otimes\delta_{\epsilon}\ \pitchfork\ X\,.

(2)

The canonical maps
(\delta_{\epsilon}\Rightarrow{X}):X^{I}\rightarrow X
are trivial fibrations.

(3)

X\rightarrow 1
admits a
uniform biased fibration structure
. Explicitly, for each basic biased trivial cofibration
c\otimes\delta_{\epsilon}:B\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{n+1}
and map
x:B\rightarrow X
, there is given an extension
j_{\epsilon}(c,x)
,

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\otimes\delta_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X,}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}^{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\epsilon}(c,x)}

(36)

and, moreover, the choice is
uniform in
\mathrm{I}^{n}
in the following sense: Given any cubical map
u:\mathrm{I}^{m}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{n}
, the pullback
u^{*}c\otimes\delta_{\epsilon}:B^{\prime}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{m}\times\mathrm{I}
fits into a commutative diagram of the form

\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}c\otimes\delta_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{(u\times\mathrm{I})^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\otimes\delta_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X.}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}^{m}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u\times\mathrm{I}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}^{n}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j(c,x)}

(37)

For the pair
(u^{*}c\otimes\delta_{\epsilon},\,x\circ(u\times\mathrm{I})^{\prime})
in (
37
) the chosen extension

j(u^{*}c\otimes\delta_{\epsilon},x\circ(u\times\mathrm{I})^{\prime}):\mathrm{I}^{m}\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrow X

is equal to
j(c,x)\circ(u\times\mathrm{I})
,

j(u^{*}c\otimes\delta_{\epsilon},x\circ(u\times\mathrm{I})^{\prime})=j(c,x)(u\times\mathrm{I}).

(38)

Partial box filling (unbiased version)

Rather than building a weak factorization system based on the foregoing notion of biased fibration (as is done in
[
GS17
]
), we shall first eliminate the “bias” with respect to the endpoints
\delta_{\epsilon}:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, for
\epsilon=0,1
. This will have the effect of adding more trivial cofibrations, and thus more weak equivalences, to our model structure. Consider first the simple path-lifting condition for a map
f:Y\rightarrow X
, which is a special case of (
28
) with
c=\,!:0\rightarrowtail 1
, so that
!\otimes\delta_{\epsilon}=\delta_{\epsilon}
.

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\epsilon}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\epsilon}}
\textstyle{X}

In topological spaces, for instance, rather than requiring lifts
j_{\epsilon}
for each of the endpoints
\epsilon=0,1
of the real interval
\mathrm{I}=[0,1]
, one could equivalently require there to be a lift
j_{i}

for each point

i:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
. Such “unbiased path-lifting” can be formulated in
\mathsf{cSet}
by introducing a “generic point”
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
by passing to
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
via the pullback functor
\mathrm{I}^{*}:\mathsf{cSet}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
, and then requiring path-lifting for
\mathrm{I}^{*}f
with respect to
\delta:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}\times\mathrm{I}
, regarded as a map
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{*}{\mathrm{I}}
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
. We shall therefore define
f
to be an unbiased fibration just if
\mathrm{I}^{*}f
is a
\delta
-biased fibration for the generic point
\delta
. The following specification implements that idea, while also adding cofibrant partiality, as in the biased case.

We first replace axiom (C5) with the following stronger assumption.

(C7)

The diagonal map
\delta:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}\times\mathrm{I}
of the interval
\mathrm{I}
is a cofibration.

The unbiased notion of a fibration for
\mathsf{cSet}
is now as follows.

Definition 25

(unbiased fibration)
.

Let
\delta:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}\times\mathrm{I}
be the diagonal map.

(1)

An object
X
is
unbiased fibrant
if the map

(\delta\Rightarrow\!X)=\langle\mathsf{eval},p_{2}\rangle:X^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrow X\times\mathrm{I}

is a trivial fibration.

(2)

A map
f:Y\rightarrow X
is an
unbiased fibration
if the map

(\delta\Rightarrow\!f)=\langle f^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I},\langle\mathsf{eval},p_{2}\rangle\rangle:Y^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrow(X^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I})\times_{(X\times\mathrm{I})}(Y\times\mathrm{I})

is a trivial fibration.

Let us (temporarily) write
\mathbb{I}=\mathrm{I}^{*}\mathrm{I}
for the pulled-back interval in the slice category
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
, so that the generic point is written
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathbb{I}
.
Condition (
1
) above (which of course is a special case of (
2
)) then says that evaluation at the generic point
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathbb{I}
, the map
(\mathrm{I}^{*}X)^{\delta}:(\mathrm{I}^{*}X)^{\mathbb{I}}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{*}X
, constructed in the slice category
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
, is a trivial fibration.
Condition (
2
) says that the pullback-hom of the generic point
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathbb{I}
with
\mathrm{I}^{*}f
, constructed in the slice category
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
, is a trivial fibration. Thus a map
f:Y\rightarrow X
is an
unbiased
fibration just if its base change
\mathrm{I}^{*}f
is a
\delta
-
biased
fibration in the slice category
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
. The latter condition can also be reformulated as follows.

Proposition 26
.

A map
f:Y\rightarrow X
is an unbiased fibration if and only if the canonical map
u
to the pullback, in the following diagram in
\mathsf{cSet}
, is a trivial fibration.

\textstyle{Y^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{eval}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I}}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{Y_{\mathsf{eval}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{eval}}
\textstyle{X.}

(39)

Proof.

We interpolate another pullback into the rectangle in (
39
) to obtain

\textstyle{Y_{\mathsf{eval}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X}

(40)

with the evident maps. The left hand square is therefore a pullback, so we indeed have that

Y_{\mathsf{eval}}\ \cong\ (X^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I})\times_{(X\times\mathrm{I})}(Y\times\mathrm{I})\cong\ (X^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I})\times_{X}Y

and
u=(\delta\Rightarrow\!f)
.
∎

As a special case, we have:

Corollary 27
.

An object
X
is unbiased fibrant if and only if the canonical map
u
to the pullback, in the following diagram in
\mathsf{cSet}
, is a trivial fibration.

\textstyle{X^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{eval}}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1.}

(41)

Now we can run the proof of Proposition
22
backwards in order to determine a class of generating trivial cofibrations for the unbiased case. Consider pairs of maps
c:C\rightarrowtail Z
and
i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, where the former is a cofibration and the latter is regarded as an “
\mathrm{I}
-indexing”, so that

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}}

is regarded as an “
\mathrm{I}
-indexed family of cofibrations
c_{i}:C_{i}\rightarrowtail Z_{i}
”. We shall use the notation

\langle i\rangle:=\langle 1_{Z},i\rangle:Z\longrightarrow Z\times\mathrm{I}\,,

(42)

for the graph of the indexing map
i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
. Then write

c\otimes_{i}\!\delta:=[\langle i\rangle,c\times\mathrm{I}]:Z+_{C}(C\times\mathrm{I})\longrightarrow Z\times\mathrm{I}\,,

which is easily seen to be well-defined on the indicated pushout below.

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{\langle ic\rangle}
\textstyle{C\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\times\mathrm{I}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i\rangle}
\textstyle{Z+_{C}(C\times\mathrm{I})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\,\otimes_{i}\delta}
\textstyle{Z\times\mathrm{I}\,.}

(43)

Remark 28
.

The specification (
43
) differs from the similar (
27
) by using the graph
\langle i\rangle:Z\rightarrowtail Z\times\mathrm{I}
for the inclusion of
Z
into the cylinder over
Z
, rather than one of the two “ends”,

\langle 1_{Z},\delta_{\epsilon}!\rangle:Z\cong Z\times 1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Z\times\delta_{\epsilon}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Z\times\mathrm{I}

(44)

arising from the endpoint inclusions
\delta_{\epsilon}:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, for
\epsilon=0,1
. As an arrow over
\mathrm{I}
, the graph
\langle i\rangle:Z\rightarrow Z\times\mathrm{I}
also takes the form (
44
), namely

\langle i\rangle=\langle 1_{Z},\delta!\rangle:Z\rightarrow Z\times\mathrm{I}\,.

If we also regard
c:C\rightarrow Z
as an arrow over
\mathrm{I}
via
i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, and use the generic point
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathbb{I}
over
\mathrm{I}
in place of
\delta_{\epsilon}:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, then (
43
) agrees with (
27
), up to those changes. Thus the indicated map
{c\,\otimes_{i}\delta}
in (
43
)
is the pushout-product constructed over
\mathrm{I}
of the generic point
\delta
with the map
c
regarded as an
\mathrm{I}
-indexed family of cofibrations via the indexing
i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
.

Observe that for any map
i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, the graph
\langle i\rangle=\langle 1_{Z},i\rangle:Z\rightarrow Z\times\mathrm{I}
is a cofibration, since it is a pullback of the diagonal of
\mathrm{I}
along
i\times\mathrm{I}
. The subobject

c\otimes_{i}\!\delta\rightarrowtail Z\times\mathrm{I}

constructed in (
43
) is therefore a cofibration, since it is the join in the lattice
\mathsf{Sub}{(}Z\times\mathrm{I})
of the cofibrant subobjects
\langle i\rangle\rightarrowtail Z\times\mathrm{I}
and
C\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrowtail Z\times\mathrm{I}
, where the latter is the “cylinder over
C\rightarrowtail Z
”.

Definition 29
.

The maps of the form
c\otimes_{i}\delta:Z+_{C}(C\times\mathrm{I})\rightarrowtail Z\times\mathrm{I}
now form the class of
generating unbiased trivial cofibrations
,

\mathcal{C}\otimes\delta\ =\ \{c\otimes_{i}\delta:D\rightarrowtail Z\times\mathrm{I}\ |\ c:C\rightarrowtail Z,i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}\}\,.

(45)

We can then show that the unbiased fibrations are exactly the right class of these maps,

(\mathcal{C}\otimes\delta)^{\pitchfork}=\mathcal{F}.

Proposition 30
.

A map
f:Y\rightarrow X
is an unbiased fibration iff for every pair of maps
c:C\rightarrowtail Z
and
i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, where the former is a cofibration, every commutative square of the following form has a diagonal filler, as indicated in the following.

\textstyle{Z+_{C}(C\times\mathrm{I})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\,\otimes_{i}\,\delta}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Z\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{X.}

(46)

Proof.

Suppose that for all
c:C\rightarrowtail Z
and
i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, we have
(c\otimes_{i}\delta)\pitchfork f
in
\mathsf{cSet}
. Pulling
f
back over
\mathrm{I}
, this is equivalent to the condition
c\otimes\delta\pitchfork\mathrm{I}^{*}f
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
, for all cofibrations
c:C\rightarrowtail Z
over
\mathrm{I}
, which is equivalent to
c\pitchfork(\delta\Rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{*}f)
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
for all cofibrations
c:C\rightarrowtail Z
. But this in turn means that
\delta\Rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{*}f
is a trivial fibration, which by definition means that
f
is an unbiased fibration.
∎

Remark 31
.

Note that the endpoints
\delta_{\epsilon}:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, in particular, are of the form
c\otimes_{i}\!\delta
by taking
Z=1
and
i=\delta_{\epsilon}
and
c=\ !:0\rightarrow 1
, so that the case of biased filling is subsumed. Moreover, for any
i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
the graph
\langle i\rangle:Z\rightarrowtail Z\times\mathrm{I}
is itself of the form
0\otimes_{i}\!\delta
for the cofibration
0\rightarrowtail Z
, so the graph of any “
\mathrm{I}
-indexing” map
i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
is also a trivial cofibration.

The following sanity check will be needed later.

Proposition 32
.

Let
f:F\twoheadrightarrow X
be an unbiased fibration in
\mathsf{cSet}
. Then for the endpoints
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, the associated pullback-homs,

\delta_{\epsilon}\Rightarrow f:F^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow X^{\mathrm{I}}\times_{X}F\qquad(\epsilon=0,1)

(47)

are also trivial fibrations. Thus unbiased fibrations are also
\delta_{\epsilon}
-biased fibrations, for
\epsilon=0,1
.

Proof.

This follows from Remark
31
and the
{\otimes}\dashv{\Rightarrow}
adjunction, but we give a different proof.
Consider the case
X=1
, the general one
f:F\rightarrow X
being analogous. Thus let
F
be an unbiased fibrant object in
\mathsf{cSet}
. So by definition
(\mathrm{I}^{*}F)^{\delta}:(\mathrm{I}^{*}F)^{\mathbb{I}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{I}^{*}F
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
is a trivial fibration.
Pulling back
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathbb{I}
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
along the base change
\delta_{\epsilon}:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
takes it to
\delta_{\epsilon}:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
in
\mathsf{cSet}
, by the universal property of the generic point
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathbb{I}
; that is
\delta_{\epsilon}^{*}(\delta)=\delta_{\epsilon}:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
. So
(\mathrm{I}^{*}F)^{\delta}:(\mathrm{I}^{*}F)^{\mathbb{I}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{I}^{*}F
is taken by
\delta_{\epsilon}^{*}
to

\delta_{\epsilon}^{*}\big({(\mathrm{I}^{*}F)^{\delta}}\big)=(\delta_{\epsilon}^{*}\mathrm{I}^{*}F)^{\delta_{\epsilon}^{*}\delta}=F^{\delta_{\epsilon}}:F^{\mathrm{I}}\longrightarrow F\,,

as shown in the following.

\textstyle{F^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\delta_{\epsilon}}}
\textstyle{(\mathrm{I}^{*}F)^{\mathbb{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{I}^{*}F)^{\delta}}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}^{*}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\epsilon}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

(48)

And pullback preserves trivial fibrations.
∎

Unbiased fibration structure.

As in the biased case, the fibrations can be determined by
uniform
right-lifting against a
small category
of unbiased trivial cofibrations, now consisting of all those
c\otimes_{i}\delta
in
\mathcal{C}\otimes\delta
for which
c:C\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{n}
is basic,
i.e.
has representable codomain. Call these maps the
basic unbiased trivial cofibrations
, and let

\mathsf{BCof}\otimes\delta=\{c\otimes_{i}\delta:B\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{n+1}\ |\ c:C\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{n},\,i:\mathrm{I}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{I},\,n\geq 0\}\,,

(49)

where the pushout-product
c\otimes_{i}\delta
now has the form

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{\langle ic\rangle}
\textstyle{C\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\times\mathrm{I}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i\rangle}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}^{n}+_{C}(C\times\mathrm{I})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\,\otimes_{i}\delta}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}^{n}\times\mathrm{I}}

(50)

for a basic cofibration
c:C\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{n}
and an indexing map
i:\mathrm{I}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, and with domain
B=\big(\mathrm{I}^{n}+_{C}(C\times\mathrm{I})\big)
. These subobjects
B\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{n+1}
can again be seen geometrically as “generalized open box inclusions", but now the floor and lid of the open box are generalized to the graph of an arbitrary map
i:\mathrm{I}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
.

For any map
f:Y\rightarrow X
a uniform, unbiased fibration structure on
f
is then a choice of diagonal fillers
j(c,i,x,y)
,

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\,\otimes_{i}\delta}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}^{n}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j(c,i,x,y)}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{Y,}

(51)

for each basic trivial cofibration
c\otimes_{i}\delta:B\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{n+1}
, which is
uniform in
\mathrm{I}^{n}
in the following sense: Given any cubical map
u:\mathrm{I}^{m}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{n}
, the pullback
u^{*}c:u^{*}C\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{m}
and the reindexing
iu:\mathrm{I}^{m}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
determine another basic trivial cofibration
u^{*}c\otimes_{iu}\delta:B^{\prime}=(\mathrm{I}^{m}+_{u^{*}C}(u^{*}C\times\mathrm{I}))\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{m+1}
, which fits into a commutative diagram of the form

\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}c\,\otimes_{iu}\delta}
\scriptstyle{(u\times\mathrm{I})^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\,\otimes_{i}\delta}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}^{m}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u\times\mathrm{I}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}^{n}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j(c,i,x,y)}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{Y.}

(52)

For the outer rectangle in (
52
) there is a chosen diagonal filler

j(u^{*}c,iu,x(u\times\mathrm{I})^{\prime},y(u\times\mathrm{I})):\mathrm{I}^{m}\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrow X,

and for this map we require that

j(u^{*}c,iu,x(u\times\mathrm{I})^{\prime},y(u\times\mathrm{I}))=j(c,i,x,y)\circ(u\times\mathrm{I}).

(53)

Definition 33
.

A
uniform, unbiased fibration structure
on a map

f:Y\rightarrow X

is a choice of fillers
j(c,i,x,y)
as in (
51
) satisfying (
53
) for all cubical maps
u:\mathrm{I}^{m}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{n}
.

In these terms, we have the following analogue of corollary
24
.

Proposition 34
.

For any object
X
in
\mathsf{cSet}
the following are equivalent:

(1)

X
is an unbiased fibrant object in the sense of Definition
25
: the canonical map
\delta\Rightarrow X:X^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrow X\times\mathrm{I}
is a trivial fibration.

(2)

X
has the right lifting property with respect to all generating unbiased trivial cofibrations,

(\mathcal{C}\otimes\delta)\,\pitchfork\,X.

(3)

X
has a uniform, unbiased fibration structure in the sense of Definition
33
.

Proof.

The equivalence between (1) and (2) is proposition
30
.
So assume (1).
Then in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
, the evaluation at
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathbb{I}
,

(\mathrm{I}^{*}X)^{\delta}:(\mathrm{I}^{*}X)^{\mathbb{I}}\longrightarrow X

is a trivial fibration. By Proposition
17
it therefore
has a uniform filling structure with respect to all basic cofibrations
c:C\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{n}
over
\mathrm{I}
. Transposing by the
\otimes\dashv\,\Rightarrow
adjunction and unwinding then gives exactly a uniform fibration structure on
X
.
∎

A statement analogous to the foregoing also holds for maps
f:Y\rightarrow X
in place of objects
X
. Indeed, as before, we have the following sharper formulation.

Corollary 35
.

Uniform, unbiased fibration structures on a map
f:Y\rightarrow X
correspond uniquely to relative
+
-algebra structures on the map
(\delta\Rightarrow{f})
(cf. definition
25
),

(\delta\Rightarrow{f}):Y^{I}\times\mathrm{I}\longrightarrow(X^{I}\times\mathrm{I})\times_{(X\times\mathrm{I})}(Y\times\mathrm{I})\,.

Factorization

Definition 36
.

Summarizing the foregoing definitions, we have the following classes of maps:

•

The
generating unbiased trivial cofibrations
were determined in (
45
) as

\mathcal{C}\otimes\delta=\{c\otimes_{i}\delta:D\rightarrowtail Z\times\mathrm{I}\ |\ c:C\rightarrowtail Z,\,i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}\}\,,

(54)

where
D=\big(Z+_{C}(C\times\mathrm{I})\big)
and the pushout-product
c\otimes_{i}\delta
has the form

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{\langle ic\rangle}
\textstyle{C\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\times\mathrm{I}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle ic\rangle}
\textstyle{Z+_{C}(C\times\mathrm{I})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c\,\otimes_{i}\delta}
\textstyle{Z\times\mathrm{I}}

(55)

for any cofibration
c:C\rightarrowtail Z
and indexing map
i:Z\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
.

•

The class
\mathcal{F}
of
unbiased fibrations
, which can be characterized as the right-lifting class of the generating unbiased trivial cofibrations,

(\mathcal{C}\otimes\delta)^{\pitchfork}\,=\,\mathcal{F}.

•

The class of
unbiased trivial cofibrations
is then defined to be left-lifting class of the fibrations,

\mathsf{TCof}\,=\,^{\pitchfork}\mathcal{F}.

It follows that the classes
\mathsf{TCof}
and
\mathcal{F}
are closed under retracts and are mutually weakly orthogonal,
\mathsf{TCof}\ {\pitchfork}\ \mathcal{F}
.
Thus in order to have a weak factorization system
(\mathsf{TCof},\mathcal{F})
it just remains to show the following.

Lemma 37
.

Every map
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathsf{cSet}
can be factored as
f=p\circ i
,

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{Y}

(56)

with
i:X\rightarrowtail X^{\prime}
an unbiased trivial cofibration and
p:X^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow Y
an unbiased fibration.

Proof.

We can use a standard argument (the “algebraic small object argument”, cf.
[
Gar09
,
Rie11
]
), which can be further simplified using the fact that the codomains of the basic trivial cofibrations
c\,\otimes_{i}\,\delta:B\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}^{n+1}
are not just representable, but
tiny
in the sense of Proposition
4
, and the domains are not merely “small”, but
finitely presented
. The reader is referred to
[
Awo18
]
for details in a similar case.
∎

Remark 38
.

The proof in
ibid.
actually produces a stronger result than we need, namely an
algebraic
weak factorization system. This follows from the small generating
category

\mathsf{BCof}\otimes\delta
of basic unbiased trivial cofibrations (and pullback squares of the form on the left in (
52
)). The relationship between this stronger condition and the
classifying types
used in Section
6
is studied in
[
Swa18
]
, which also gives an even more “constructive” proof of the factorization Lemma
37
, not requiring quotients, exactness, or impredicativity. With this modification, the present approach can also be used in a
quasi
topos, as occurs in
e.g.
realizability and sheaves.

Proposition 39
.

There is a weak factorization system on the category
\mathsf{cSet}
in which the right maps are the unbiased fibrations and the left maps are the unbiased trivial cofibrations, both as specified in definition
36
. This will be called the
(unbiased) fibration weak factorization system
.

Hereafter, unless otherwise stated, all fibrations in
\mathsf{cSet}
are assumed to be unbiased.

4.
The weak equivalences

Our approach to proving that the classes
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{F}
of cofibrations and fibrations, from Sections
2
and
3
, determine a model structure will be to first identify a
premodel structure
in the sense of
[
Bar19
]
, and then turn to the question of the 3-for-2 property for the resulting weak equivalences.

Definition 40

(Weak equivalence)
.

A map
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathsf{cSet}
is a
weak equivalence
if it can be factored as
f=g\circ h
,

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y}

with
h\pitchfork\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{C}\pitchfork g
. Accordingly, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{TFib}\circ\mathsf{TCof}

\displaystyle=\{f:X\rightarrow Y\,|\ f=g\circ h\ \text{for some $g\in\mathsf{TFib}$ and $h\in\mathsf{TCof}$}\}

be the class of weak equivalences.

Observe first that every trivial fibration
f\in\mathsf{TFib}=\mathcal{C}^{\pitchfork}
is indeed a fibration, because the generating trivial cofibrations
c\otimes_{i}\delta
are cofibrations. Moreover, every trivial fibration
f:X\rightarrow Y
is also a weak equivalence
f=f\circ 1_{X}
, since the identity map
1_{X}
is (trivially) a trivial cofibration
\mathsf{TCof}=\,^{\pitchfork}\mathcal{F}
. Thus we have

\mathsf{TFib}\subseteq(\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{W}).

Similarly, because
\mathsf{TFib}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
, we have
\mathsf{TCof}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, since identity maps are also trivial fibrations we have
\mathsf{TCof}\subseteq\mathsf{TFib}\circ\mathsf{TCof}=\mathcal{W}
. Thus we also have

\mathsf{TCof}\subseteq(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{W}).

Lemma 41
.

(\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{W})\subseteq\mathsf{TCof}.

Proof.

Let
c:A\rightarrowtail B
be a cofibration with a factorization

c=tf\circ tc:A\rightarrow W\rightarrow B

where
tc\in\mathsf{TCof}
and
tf\in\mathsf{TFib}
. Let
f:X\twoheadrightarrow Y
be a fibration and consider a commutative diagram,

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{Y.}

Inserting the factorization of
c
, from
tc\pitchfork f
we obtain
j:W\rightarrow X
as indicated, with
j\circ tc=x
and
f\circ j=y\circ tf
.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{tc}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{tf}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{Y.}

Moreover, since
c\pitchfork tf
there is an
i:B\rightarrow W
as indicated, with
i\circ c=tc
and
tf\circ i=1_{B}
.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{tc}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{tf}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{Y.}

Let
k=j\circ i
. Then
k\circ c=j\circ i\circ c=j\circ tc=x
, and
f\circ k=f\circ j\circ i=y\circ tf\circ i=y
.
∎

The proof of the following is exactly dual.

Lemma 42
.

(\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{W})\subseteq\mathsf{TFib}.

Proposition 43
.

The three classes of maps
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{F}
in
\mathsf{cSet}
constitute a
premodel structure
in the sense of
[
Bar19
]
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\cap\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{TFib},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{TCof},

and therefore two interlocking weak factorization systems:

(\mathcal{C},\,\mathcal{W}\cap\mathcal{F})\ \ ,\ \ (\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{W},\,\mathcal{F}).

It now “only” remains to show that the weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
satisfy the 3-for-2 axiom from Definition
1
in order to verify that
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{F})
is a model structure. Perhaps surprisingly, this will occupy the remainder of these lectures! We shall follow roughly the approach of
[
JT08
]
: the weak equivalences between fibrant objects are shown to be the usual
homotopy equivalences
, which evidently satisfy 3-for-2. So we reduce to this case using the fact that
K^{X}
is fibrant whenever
K
is. It suffices, namely, to show that the weak equivalences are those maps
w:X\rightarrow Y
that induce homotopy equivalences
K^{w}:K^{Y}\simeq K^{X}
for fibrant
K
. Such maps are termed
weak homotopy equivalences
(Definition
51
), and our task will therefore be to show that a map is a weak equivalence if and only if it is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Homotopy equivalence.

Definition 44

(Homotopy)
.

A
homotopy

\vartheta:f\sim g
between maps
f,g:X\rightrightarrows Y
is a map,

\vartheta:\mathrm{I}\times{X}\longrightarrow Y,

such that
\vartheta\circ\iota_{0}=f
and
\vartheta\circ\iota_{1}=g
,

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}\times\!{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{X,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y}

(57)

where
\iota_{0},\iota_{1}
are the canonical inclusions into the ends of the cylinder,

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 33.67213pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-33.67213pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\iota_{\epsilon}:X\cong 1\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.39485pt\raise 5.93056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.93056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{\epsilon}\times X}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.67213pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 57.67213pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 87.67213pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{I}\times X\,,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\epsilon=0,1.

Note that each of the inclusions
\iota_{\epsilon}:X\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}\times X
is a cofibration, as is their join
X+X\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}\times X
, by Remark
20
.

Proposition 45
.

The relation of homotopy
f\sim g
between maps
f,g:X\rightrightarrows Y
is preserved by pre- and post-composition. If
Y
is fibrant, then
f\sim g
is an equivalence relation.

Proof.

Inspecting (
57
), preservation of
f\sim g
under post-composing with any
h:Y\rightarrow Z
is obvious: we have
h\circ\vartheta:h\circ\,f\sim h\circ\,g
. Now observe that a homotopy
f\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartheta}}{{\sim}}g:X\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrow Y
determines a (unique) path
\tilde{\vartheta}:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow Y^{X}
in the function space, with endpoints
\vartheta_{0}=\vartheta\circ\delta_{0}=\tilde{f}:1\rightarrow Y^{X}
and
\vartheta_{1}=\vartheta\circ\delta_{1}=\tilde{g}
.
Precomposing maps
f,g:X\rightrightarrows Y
with any
e:W\rightarrow X
is induced by post-composing
\tilde{f},\tilde{g}:1\rightarrow Y^{X}
with the map
Y^{e}:Y^{X}\rightarrow Y^{W}
, which then also takes the path
\tilde{\vartheta}:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow Y^{X}
to a path
\tilde{\varphi}=Y^{e}\circ\tilde{\vartheta}:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow Y^{W}
corresponding to a (unique) homotopy
\varphi:f\circ e\sim g\circ e
.

Now note that
Y^{X}
is fibrant if
Y
is fibrant, since the generating trivial cofibrations
c\times_{i}\delta
are preserved by the functor
X\times(-)
. So we can use “box-filling” in
Y^{X}
to verify the claimed equivalence relation.

•

Reflexivity
f\sim f
is witnessed by the homotopy
\rho:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow 1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}Y^{X}
.

•

For symmetry
f\sim g\Rightarrow g\sim f
take
\vartheta:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow Y^{X}
with
\vartheta_{0}=f
and
\vartheta_{1}=g
and we want to build
\vartheta^{\prime}:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow Y^{X}
with
\vartheta^{\prime}_{0}=g
and
\vartheta^{\prime}_{1}=f
. Take an open 2-box in
Y^{X}
of the following form.

\textstyle{g}
\textstyle{f}
\textstyle{f\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{f\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}

This box is a map
b:\mathrm{I}+_{1}\mathrm{I}+_{1}\mathrm{I}\rightarrow Y^{X}
with the indicated components, and it has a filler
c:\mathrm{I}\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrow Y^{X}
, i.e. an extension along the canonical map
\mathrm{I}+_{1}\mathrm{I}+_{1}\mathrm{I}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}\times\mathrm{I}
, which is a trivial cofibration of the form
\partial{\mathrm{I}}\otimes\delta_{0}
. Let
t:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}\times\mathrm{I}
be the top face of the 2-cube (the bipointed map
\{0,x_{1},x_{2},1\}\rightarrow\{0,x,1\}
that is constantly
1
). We can set
\vartheta^{\prime}=c\circ t:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow Y^{X}
to get a homotopy
\vartheta^{\prime}:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow Y^{X}
with
\vartheta^{\prime}_{0}=g
and
\vartheta^{\prime}_{1}=f
as required.

•

For transitivity,
f\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartheta}}{{\sim}}g\,,\,g\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\sim}}h\Rightarrow f\sim h
, an analogous construction will fill the open box:

\textstyle{f}
\textstyle{h}
\textstyle{f\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{g\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}

∎

We then have the usual definition of homotopy equivalence:

Definition 46

(Homotopy equivalence)
.

A
homotopy equivalence
is a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
together with a map
g:Y\rightarrow X
and homotopies
\vartheta:1_{X}\sim g\circ f
and
\varphi:1_{Y}\sim f\circ g
. We call
g
a
quasi-inverse
of
f
.

Since these maps clearly compose and come with quasi-inverses, the following is then immediate.

Lemma 47
.

The homotopy equivalences satisfy the 3-for-2 condition.

Lemma 48
.

A fibration that is a weak equivalence is a homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Any trivial fibration
f:X\twoheadrightarrow Y
has a section
s:Y\rightarrow X
by Corollary
18
.
Consider the following lifting problem:

\textstyle{X+X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\iota_{0},\iota_{1}]}
\scriptstyle{[sf,1]}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{Y}

Since the map on the left is a cofibration, a diagonal filler provides a homotopy
\vartheta:sf\sim 1_{X}
.
Thus
f
is a homotopy equivalence.
∎

For the further comparison of the weak equivalences with the homotopy equivalences we need the following.

Weak homotopy equivalence.

Definition 49

(Connected components)
.

The functor

\pi_{0}:\mathsf{cSet}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}

is defined on a cubical set
X
as the coequalizer

X_{1}\rightrightarrows X_{0}\rightarrow\pi_{0}X\,,

where the two parallel arrows are the maps
X_{\delta_{0}},X_{\delta_{1}}:X_{1}\rightrightarrows X_{0}
for the endpoints
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}:1\rightrightarrows\mathrm{I}
. If
K
is fibrant, then by the foregoing Proposition
45
, for any
X
we have

\pi_{0}(K^{X})=\mathrm{Hom}(X,K)/\!\!\sim\,.

That is,
\pi_{0}(K^{X})
is the set
[X,K]
of homotopy equivalence classes of maps
X\rightarrow K
.

Remark 50
.

One can show that in fact
\pi_{0}X=\varinjlim X_{n}
where the colimit is taken over
all
objects
[n]
in the index category
{\Box}^{\mathrm{op}}=\mathbb{B}
, rather than just the “last” two
[1]\rightrightarrows[0]
. Since the category
\mathbb{B}
of finite strictly bipointed sets is sifted,
the functor
\pi_{0}:\mathsf{cSet}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
preserves finite products.

Definition 51

(Weak homotopy equivalence)
.

A map
f:X\rightarrow Y
is called a
weak homotopy equivalence
if for every fibrant object
K
, the canonical map
K^{f}:K^{Y}\rightarrow K^{X}
is bijective on connected components,

\pi_{0}(K^{f}):\pi_{0}(K^{Y})\cong\pi_{0}(K^{X})\,.

Lemma 52
.

Every homotopy equivalence is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a homotopy equivalence. Then
K^{f}:K^{Y}\rightarrow K^{X}
is also a homotopy equivalence for any
K
, since homotopy respects (post-) composition by all maps. If
K
is fibrant, then so is
K^{X}
and
\pi_{0}
is well defined on homotopy classes of maps, by Proposition
45
. It clearly takes homotopy equivalences to isomorphisms of sets, since it identifies homotopic maps.
∎

Lemma 53
.

The weak homotopy equivalences also satisfy the 3-for-2 condition.

Proof.

This follows by applying the
\mathsf{Set}
-valued functors
\pi_{0}(K^{(-)})
, for all fibrant objects
K
, and the corresponding fact about bijections of sets.
∎

In virtue of Lemma
53
it now suffices to show that a map is a weak equivalence if and only if it is a weak homotopy equivalence. The following characterization will be useful.

Lemma 54
.

A map
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a weak homotopy equivalence just if it satisfies the following two conditions.

(1)

For every fibrant object
K
and every map
x:X\rightarrow K
there is a map
y:Y\rightarrow K
such that
y\circ f\sim x
,

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{K.}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

We say that
x
“extends along
f
up to homotopy”.

(2)

For every fibrant object
K
and maps
y,y^{\prime}:Y\rightarrow K
such that
yf\sim y^{\prime}f
, there is a homotopy
y\sim y^{\prime}
,

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{K^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle y,y^{\prime}\rangle}
\textstyle{K\times K.}

Proof.

Condition (1) says exactly that the internal precomposition map
K^{f}:K^{Y}\rightarrow K^{X}
is surjective under connected components
\pi_{0}
, while (2) says just that it is injective under
\pi_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 55
.

Any weak equivalence is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

By Lemma
48
and Lemma
52
, a trivial fibration is also a weak homotopy equivalence. So it suffices to consider the trivial cofibrations, since weak homotopy equivalences are closed under composition, by Lemma
53
. Thus let
f:X\rightarrowtail Y
be a trivial cofibration, and apply Lemma
54
: condition (1) is immediate, and (2) follows because
K^{\mathrm{I}}\twoheadrightarrow K\times K
is a fibration when
K
is fibrant, since
\partial:1+1\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}
is a cofibration (by Remark
20
).
∎

Our goal is now to show the converse of Lemma
55
(2), that a weak homotopy equivalence is a weak equivalence. We shall first restrict attention to maps
f:X\rightarrow K
with a fibrant codomain
K
. By factoring such maps, we can split into the cases of a fibration and a cofibration.

Lemma 56
.

If
K
is fibrant, then any fibration
f:X\twoheadrightarrow K
that is a homotopy equivalence is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

This is a standard argument, which we just sketch. It suffices to show that any diagram of the form

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{K,}

(58)

with
c:C\rightarrowtail X
a cofibration, has a diagonal filler, for then
f
is a trivial fibration.
Since
f
is a homotopy equivalence, it has a quasi-inverse
s:K\rightarrow X
with
\vartheta:fs\sim 1_{K}
, which we claim can be corrected to a section
s^{\prime}:K\rightarrow X
.
Indeed, consider

\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{K\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{K}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{=}

where
\vartheta^{\prime}
results from
\iota_{0}\pitchfork f
. Let
s^{\prime}=\vartheta^{\prime}\iota_{1}
, so that
\vartheta^{\prime}:s\sim s^{\prime}
and
fs^{\prime}=1_{K}
.

Thus we can assume that
s=s^{\prime}:K\rightarrow X
is a section, which fills the diagram (
58
) up to a homotopy in the upper triangle.

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{x}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{K}

Now we can correct
s:K\rightarrow X
to a homotopic
t:K\rightarrow X
over
f
by using the homotopy
\varphi:sc\sim x
to get a map
\varphi:C\rightarrow X^{\mathrm{I}}
over
f
. Since
f
is a fibration, the projections
p_{0},p_{1}:X^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow X
over
f
are trivial fibrations, and so there is a lift
\varphi^{\prime}:K\rightarrow X^{\mathrm{I}}
for which
t:=p_{1}\varphi^{\prime}
has
tc=x
and
ft=1_{K}
, and so is a filler for (
58
).
∎

Lemma 57
.

If
K
is fibrant, then any fibration
f:X\twoheadrightarrow K
that is a weak homotopy equivalence is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

Since
K
is fibrant, so is
X
, and since
f
is a weak homotopy equivalence, by lemma
54
(1) there is then a map
s:K\rightarrow X
and a homotopy
\theta:sf\sim 1_{X}
. Postcomposing with
f
gives a homotopy
f\vartheta:fsf\sim f
, forming the outer commutative square in

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{f\vartheta}
\textstyle{K^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\langle fs,1_{K}\rangle}
\textstyle{K\times K.}

By lemma
54
(2) there is a diagonal filler
\varphi:fs\sim 1_{K}
, and so
f
is a homotopy equivalence. Now apply lemma
56
.
∎

We now have the following.

Proposition 58
.

If
A
and
K
are both fibrant, then for any cofibration
c:A\rightarrowtail K
the following are equivalent.

(1)

c:A\rightarrowtail K
is a weak equivalence.

(2)

c:A\rightarrowtail K
is a homotopy equivalence.

(3)

c:A\rightarrowtail K
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Suppose (1), so
c:A\rightarrowtail K
is a trivial cofibration. Then since
A
is fibrant, it has a retraction
r:K\rightarrow A
.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{{A}.}
\textstyle{{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}

Since
K
is fibrant,
K^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow K\times K
is a fibration. So the following has a diagonal filler, which is a homotopy
1_{K}\sim cr
.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{K^{!}}
\textstyle{K^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle K^{d_{0}},K^{d_{1}}\rangle}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle 1_{K},cr\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{K\times K}

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
is Lemma
52
.

Suppose (3), that
c:A\rightarrowtail K
is a weak homotopy equivalence. Factor
c=f\circ tc
with a trivial cofibration
tc:A\rightarrowtail C
followed by a fibration
f:C\twoheadrightarrow K
. By parts (1) and (2),
tc:A\rightarrowtail C
is then a weak homotopy equivalence. By 3-for-2 for weak homotopy equivalences, Lemma
53
,
f:C\twoheadrightarrow K
is then also a weak homotopy equivalence. By Lemma
57
,
f:C\twoheadrightarrow K
is then a weak equivalence.
∎

Proposition 59
.

For fibrations
f:X\twoheadrightarrow K
with fibrant codomain
K
, all three concepts coincide: weak equivalences, weak homotopy equivalences, and homotopy equivalences.

Proof.

Let
K
be fibrant and suppose that
f:X\twoheadrightarrow K
is a weak homotopy equivalence. Then it is a weak equivalence by Lemma
57
. By Lemma
55
any fibration weak equivalence is a homotopy equivalence, and by
Lemma
52
any homotopy equivalence is a weak homotopy equivalence.
∎

Corollary 60
.

For all maps
f:X\rightarrow Y
between fibrant objects
X
and
Y
, all three concepts coincide: weak equivalence, weak homotopy equivalence, and homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Let
X
and
Y
be fibrant and factor
f=tf\circ tc
with a trivial cofibration
tc:X\rightarrowtail F
followed by a trivial fibration
tf:F\twoheadrightarrow Y
.
Then by Proposition
58
,
tc:X\rightarrowtail F
is a homotopy equivalence, and by Proposition
59
so is
tf:F\twoheadrightarrow Y
, thus
f=tf\circ tc
is a homotopy equivalence. Again by Lemma
52
, any homotopy equivalence is a weak homotopy equivalence, and weak homotopy equivalence between fibrant objects is clearly a weak equivalence, by factoring and using the foregoing Propositions
58
and
59
.
∎

Lemma 61
.

If
K
is fibrant, then any cofibration
c:A\rightarrowtail K
that is a weak homotopy equivalence is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

Let
c:A\rightarrowtail K
be a cofibration weak homotopy equivalence and factor it into a trivial cofibration
i:A\rightarrowtail Z
followed by a fibration
p:Z\twoheadrightarrow K
. By lemma
54
, any trivial cofibration is clearly a weak homotopy equivalence. So both

c
and
i
are weak homotopy equivalences, and therefore so is
p
by 3-for-2 for weak homotopy equivalences. Since
K
is fibrant,
p
is a trivial fibration by lemma
57
, and thus
c
is a weak equivalence.
∎

It now follows that a weak homotopy equivalence
f:X\rightarrow K
with a fibrant codomain is a weak equivalence. To eliminate the condition on the codomain we use the following lemma due to D.-C. Cisinski
[
Cis06
]
.

Lemma 62
.

A cofibration
c:A\rightarrowtail B
weak homotopy equivalence lifts against any fibration
f:Y\twoheadrightarrow K
with fibrant codomain.

Proof.

Let
c:A\rightarrowtail B
be a cofibration weak homotopy equivalence and
f:Y\twoheadrightarrow K
a fibration with fibrant codomain
K
, and consider a lifting problem

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{K.}

Let
\eta:B\rightarrowtail B^{\prime}
be a fibrant replacement of
B
, since
K
is fibrant,
b
extends along
\eta
to give
b^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\rightarrow K
as shown below.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{K}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b^{\prime}}

Since
\eta
is a trivial cofibration, it is a weak homotopy equivalence. So the composite
\eta c
is also a weak homotopy equivalence. But since
B^{\prime}
is fibrant,
\eta c
is then a trivial cofibration by lemma
61
. Thus there is a lift
j:B^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
, and therefore also one
k=j\eta:B\rightarrow Y
.
∎

To complete the proof that a weak homotopy equivalence is a weak equivalence, we shall make use of the following
fibration extension property
, the proof of which is deferred to section
8
.

Definition 63

(Fibration extension property)
.

For any fibration
f:Y\twoheadrightarrow X
and any trivial cofibration
\eta:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
,
there is a fibration
f^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow X^{\prime}
that pulls back to
f
along
\eta
, as shown below.

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}}

(59)

Lemma 64
.

Assuming the fibration extension property, a cofibration that lifts against every fibration
f:Y\twoheadrightarrow K
with fibrant codomain is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

Let
c:A\rightarrowtail B
be a cofibration and consider a lifting problem against an arbitrary fibration
f:Y\twoheadrightarrow X
,

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{X.}

(60)

Let
\eta:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
be a fibrant replacement, so
\eta
is a trivial cofibration and
X^{\prime}
is fibrant.
By the fibration extension property of definition
63
, there is a fibration
f^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow X^{\prime}
such that
f
is a pullback of
f^{\prime}
along
\eta
. So we can extend diagram (
60
) to obtain the following, in which the righthand square is a pullback.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\ignorespaces}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}.}

(61)

By assumption, there is a lift
j^{\prime}:B\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
with
f^{\prime}j^{\prime}=\eta b
and
j^{\prime}c=yb
. Therefore, since
f
is a pullback, there is a map
j:B\rightarrow Y
with
fj=b
and
yj=j^{\prime}
.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\ignorespaces}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}.}

(62)

Thus
yjc=j^{\prime}c=ya
. But as a trivial cofibration,
\eta
is monic, and as a pullback of
\eta
,
y
is also monic. So
jc=a
.
∎

Corollary 65
.

Assuming the fibration extension property,

(1)

a cofibration
c:A\rightarrowtail B
weak homotopy equivalence is a weak equivalence,

(2)

a fibration
f:Y\twoheadrightarrow X
weak homotopy equivalence is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

(1) follows immediately by combining the previous lemmas
62
and
64
.

For (2), factor
f:Y\twoheadrightarrow X
into a cofibration
i:Y\rightarrowtail Z
followed by a trivial fibration
p:Z\twoheadrightarrow X
. Then
f
is itself a trivial fibration if
i\pitchfork f
, for then it is a retract of
p
. Since
p
is a trivial fibration, it is a weak homotopy equivalence by Lemma
55
. Since
f
is also a weak homotopy equivalence, so is
i
by Lemma
53
. Thus
i
is a trivial cofibration by (1). Since
f
is a fibration,
i\pitchfork f
as required.
∎

We have now shown:

Proposition 66
.

Assuming the fibration extension property, a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a weak homotopy equivalence if and only if it is a weak equivalence. The weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
therefore satisfy the 3-for-2 condition.

The results of this section are summarized in the following.

Theorem 67
.

Assume the fibration weak factorization system of Definition
36
satisfies the fibration extension property of Definition
63
(as will be shown in Corollary
123
).
Then the weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
have the 3-for-2 property, and so by Proposition
43
, the classes
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{F})
form a Quillen model structure.
The weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
are the
weak homotopy equivalences
: those maps
f:X\rightarrow Y
for which
K^{f}:K^{Y}\rightarrow K^{X}
is bijective on connected components whenever
K
is fibrant.

The proof of the fibration extension property will be given in Section
8
. It uses the equivalence extension property (Section
7
), a universal fibration (Section
6
), and the Frobenius condition (Section
5
), to which we now turn.

5.
The Frobenius condition

In this section, we show that the (unbiased) fibration weak factorization system from Section
3
satisfies what has been called the
Frobenius condition
: the left maps are stable under pullback along the right maps (see
[
BG12
]
). This will imply the
right properness
of our model structure: the weak equivalences are preserved by pullback along fibrations. In the present setting, it then follows that the entire model structure is stable under such a base change. The Frobenius condition will be used in the proof of the equivalence extension property in Section
7
.

A proof of Frobenius in the related setting of cubical sets
with connections
was given in
[
GS17
]
using conventional, functorial methods. By contrast, the type theoretic approach of
[
CCHM18
]
provides a proof that is much more direct, and can also be modified to work without connections (as in
[
ABC+22
]
). That approach proves the dual fact that the
pushforward
operation, which is right adjoint to pullback and always exists in a topos, preserves fibrations when applied along a fibration. This corresponds to the type-theoretic
\Pi
-formation rule, and the proof given in
op.cit.
is entirely in type theory. It also employs a reduction of box filling (in all dimensions) to an apparently weaker condition of
Kan composition
(in all dimensions), which merely “puts a lid on" the open box, rather than filling it. This aspect of the type theoretic proof can also be described functorially, but is not used in the proof given here, and will therefore not be discussed further (see
[
LOPS18
]
for a description of Kan composition with connections, and
[
Awo19a
]
for the same without connections).

Our proof takes the approach that was used to determine the unbiased fibrations, namely we first establish the result in the
biased but generic
setting, and then transfer it to the unbiased setting by pulling back along the base change
\mathsf{cSet}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
. We first give the second step as a conditional statement.

Proposition 68
.

Suppose the
\delta
-biased fibrations in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
satisfy the Frobenius condition. Then the unbiased fibrations in
\mathsf{cSet}
also satisfy the Frobenius condition.

Proof.

This follows almost immeditely from the fact that the pullback functor
\mathrm{I}^{*}:\mathsf{cSet}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
preserves the locally cartesian closed structure, takes unbiased fibrations to
\delta
-biased ones, and reflects
\delta
-biased fibrations to unbiased ones. In detail, let unbiased fibrations
B\twoheadrightarrow A
and
A\twoheadrightarrow X
in
\mathsf{cSet}
be given, and we wish to find
C\twoheadrightarrow X
and
e:A\times_{X}C\rightarrow B
over
A
, universal in the way recalled in the diagram below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{X}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}

(63)

Take the pushforward
C:=A_{*}B\rightarrow X
, and its associated map
e:A\times_{X}C\rightarrow B
, in the locally cartesian closed category
\mathsf{cSet}
. Since fibrations are stable under (all) pullbacks, it then suffices to show that
C\rightarrow X
is a fibration.

By definition,
C\rightarrow X
is an unbiased fibration in
\mathsf{cSet}
just in case the base change
\mathrm{I}^{*}C\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{*}X
is a
\delta
-biased fibration in the slice category
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
. Since the pullback functor
\mathrm{I}^{*}:\mathsf{cSet}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
preserves all lcc structure, over
\mathrm{I}^{*}X
we have an iso,

\mathrm{I}^{*}C=\mathrm{I}^{*}(A_{*}B)\cong(\mathrm{I}^{*}A)_{*}\mathrm{I}^{*}B\,,

where the pushforward
(\mathrm{I}^{*}A)_{*}\mathrm{I}^{*}B
is taken in the topos
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
. But
\mathrm{I}^{*}B\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{*}A
and
\mathrm{I}^{*}A\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{*}X
are
\delta
-biased fibrations in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
because
B\rightarrow A
and
A\rightarrow X
were assumed to be unbiased fibrations in
\mathsf{cSet}
. Since we are assuming the Frobenius condition for
\delta
-biased fibrations in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
, the pushforward
\mathrm{I}^{*}C\cong(\mathrm{I}^{*}A)_{*}\mathrm{I}^{*}B\rightarrow\mathrm{I}^{*}X
is also a
\delta
-biased fibration, as required.
∎

Frobenius for biased fibrations.

The results proved in this section will be applied to the slice category
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
and the generic point
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}=\mathrm{I}^{*}\mathrm{I}
, but nothing depends on this particular case, and so we shall write simply
\delta:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
for a chosen pointed object in an arbitrary topos
\mathcal{E}
. (Indeed, in this section
\mathcal{E}
may even be just a locally cartesian closed category with a class of cofibrations in the sense of Appendix A.)

Recall from Definition
25
that a map
f:A\rightarrow X
is a
\delta
-biased fibration just if the map
\delta\Rightarrow f
admits a relative +-algebra structure, and is therefore a trivial fibration. The definition of the pullback-hom
\delta\!\Rightarrow\!f
is recalled below.

\textstyle{A^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\mathrm{I}}}
\scriptstyle{\delta\Rightarrow{f}}
\scriptstyle{A^{\delta}}
\textstyle{X^{\mathrm{I}}\times_{X}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{X^{\delta}}
\textstyle{X}

(64)

Let us write this condition schematically as follows:

\textstyle{A^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|}
\textstyle{A_{\epsilon}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon}
\textstyle{X}

(65)

where
\epsilon=X^{\delta}
and
A_{\epsilon}=X^{\mathrm{I}}\times_{X}A
, and the struck-through arrow indicates that it admits a +-algebra structure.

Lemma 69
.

Let
A\rightarrow X
be a
\delta
-biased fibration and
t:Y\rightarrow X
any map, then the pullback
t^{*}A\rightarrow Y
is also a
\delta
-biased fibration.

Proof.

This is clear from the fact that the
\delta
-biased fibrations can be made into the right class of a weak factorization system (by reasoning analogous to that for Proposition
39
), but it will be useful to see how the structure indicated in (
64
) is itself stable under pullback. Indeed, consider the following commutative diagram, in which the front face of the cube is the pullback in question, and the right and left sides are the respective versions of the construction in (
64
).

\textstyle{(t^{*}A)^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(t^{*}A)_{\epsilon}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{\epsilon}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t^{*}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon}
\textstyle{X^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{X}

(66)

The rear square of solid arrows is the image of the front face under the pathobject functor and is therefore also a pullback. The base commutes by the naturality of the maps
\epsilon
, as does a corresponding top square involving further such
\epsilon
’s not shown. Note that these naturality squares need not be pullbacks, but the vertical squares on the sides are, by construction. It follows that there is a dotted arrow as shown, making the resulting lower rear square commute. That lower square is then also a pullback, since the other vertical faces of the resulting cube are pullbacks, and thus finally, the upper rear square is also a pullback.

Now if
A\rightarrow X
is a
\delta
-biased fibration, then
A^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow A_{\epsilon}
is a trivial fibration, and then so is its pullback
(t^{*}A)^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow(t^{*}A)_{\epsilon}
since relative
+
-algebras are stable under pullback. Therefore the pullback
t^{*}A\rightarrow Y
is also a
\delta
-biased fibration.
∎

Remark 70
.

In this way we can show algebraically that the pullback of a
\delta
-biased fibration is again one by pulling back the structure that makes it so. In Section
6
, the pullback stability of the fibration structure will be used in the construction of a universal fibration via a closely related argument.

Lemma 71
.

Let
\alpha:A\rightarrow X
and
\beta:B\rightarrow A
be
\delta
-biased fibrations, then the composite
\alpha\circ\beta:B\rightarrow X
is also a
\delta
-biased fibration.

Proof.

Again for maps in the right class of a weak factorization system this is immediate. But let us see how the fibration structures also compose. We have the following diagram for the fibration structures on
B\rightarrow A
and
A\rightarrow X
(with obvious notation).

\textstyle{B^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|}
\textstyle{B_{\epsilon_{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|}
\textstyle{A_{\epsilon_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{X}}
\textstyle{X,}

(67)

Pulling back
B\rightarrow A
in two steps we therefore obtain the intermediate map
B_{\epsilon_{X}}\rightarrow A_{\epsilon_{X}}
indicated in the following diagram.

\textstyle{B^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|}
\textstyle{B_{\epsilon_{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{\epsilon_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|}
\textstyle{A_{\epsilon_{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{X}}
\textstyle{X}

(68)

Now use the fact that a trivial fibration structure (
i.e.
a +-algebra structure) has a canonical pullback along any map, and that two such structures have a canonical composition (cf. Remark
19
), to obtain a trivial fibration structure for the indicated composite map
B^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow B_{\epsilon_{X}}
, which is then a fibration structure for the composite
B\rightarrow A\rightarrow X
.
∎

Proposition 72

(
\delta
-Biased Frobenius)
.

If
\alpha:A\rightarrow X
and
\beta:B\rightarrow A
are
\delta
-biased fibrations, then the pushforward

Given

\scriptstyle{|}

\textstyle{B^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We wish to show that the indicated map

admits a +-algebra structure. This we will do by showing that it is a retract of a known +-algebra.

Thus the image of the unit

under

provides a map

over

\textstyle{X^{\mathrm{I}}}

The map

\bullet
For

and so by the adjunction

to which we can apply

This map

over

. Applying the functor
