(133)

We record this fact as:

Corollary 93
.

\mathsf{Fib}=\Sigma_{\mathcal{V}}\,q_{*}\mathsf{Fib}_{i}(\mathrm{I}^{*}\dot{\mathcal{V}}).

The reader may also find it illuminating to reconsider the construction of the universal small unbiased fibration in more type theoretic terms.
It was defined to be
\dot{\mathsf{Fib}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{Fib}=\mathsf{Fib}(\dot{\mathcal{V}})
, for the universal family
\dot{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
, with
\dot{\mathsf{Fib}}
the pullback of
\dot{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
along the canonical projection
\mathsf{Fib}(\dot{\mathcal{V}})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
. Since, type theoretically, we have
\dot{\mathcal{V}}=\Sigma_{A:\mathcal{V}}A
, by the stability of the classifying type
\mathsf{Fib}(-)
we can write
\mathsf{Fib}=\Sigma_{A:\mathcal{V}}\mathsf{Fib}(A)
so that:

\dot{\mathsf{Fib}}=\Sigma_{A:\mathcal{V}}\mathsf{Fib}(A)\times A\ \longrightarrow\ \Sigma_{A:\mathcal{V}}\mathsf{Fib}(A)\ =\ \mathsf{Fib}\,.

Realignment for fibration structure

The realignment for families of Proposition
81
will need to be extended to (structured) fibrations. Our approach makes use of the notion of a
weak proposition
. Informally, a map
P\rightarrow X
may be said to be a weak proposition if it is “conditionally contractible”, in the sense that it is contractible if it has a section (recall that a proposition may be defined as a fibration that is “contractible if inhabited”). More formally, we have the following.

Definition 94
.

A map
P\rightarrow X
is said to be a
weak proposition
if the projection
P\times_{X}P\rightarrow P
is a trivial fibration.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

(134)

Note that if either projection is a trivial fibration, then both are.

As an object over the base, a weak proposition is thus one that “thinks it is contractible”. The key fact needed for realignment is the following.

Lemma 95
.

For any
A\rightarrow X
, the classifying type
\mathsf{TFib}(A)\rightarrow X
is a weak proposition. Moreover, the same is true for
\mathsf{Fib}(A)\rightarrow X
(both the biased and unbiased versions) if the cofibrations are closed under exponentiation by the interval
\mathrm{I}
.

Proof.

Let
A\rightarrow X
and consider the following diagram, in which we have written
A^{\prime}=\mathsf{TFib}(A)\times_{X}A
and
\mathsf{TFib}(A)^{2}=\mathsf{TFib}(A)\times_{X}\mathsf{TFib}(A)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{TFib}(A)^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{TFib}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{TFib}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

(135)

Since
\mathsf{TFib}
is stable under pullback (by Lemma
85
), we have
\mathsf{TFib}(A)^{2}\cong\mathsf{TFib}(A^{\prime})
, and since
\mathsf{TFib}(A)^{2}
has a canonical section,
A^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathsf{TFib}(A)
is therefore a trivial fibration. Inspecting the definition of
\mathsf{TFib}(A)=+\mathsf{Alg}(A)
in (
110
), we see that if a map
A\rightarrow X
is a trivial fibration, then so is
\mathsf{TFib}(A)\rightarrow X
(since
\eta:A\rightarrow A^{+}
is always a cofibration). Thus
\mathsf{TFib}(A)^{2}\cong\mathsf{TFib}(A^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathsf{TFib}(A)
is also a trivial fibration.

For
\mathsf{Fib}(A)\rightarrow X
, with reference to the construction (
124
) we use the foregoing to infer that
\mathsf{TFib}(\epsilon_{A})\rightarrow A_{\epsilon}
is a weak proposition, and so therefore is its pushforward
F_{A}=(p_{\epsilon})_{*}\mathsf{TFib}(\epsilon_{A})\rightarrow X^{\mathrm{I}}
along the projection
p_{\epsilon}:A_{\epsilon}=X^{I}\times_{X}A\rightarrow X^{\mathrm{I}}
, since pushforward clearly preserves weak propositions. Applying the root
(-)_{\mathrm{I}}
preserves trivial fibrations, by the assumption that its left adjoint
(-)^{\mathrm{I}}
preserves cofibrations, and so, as a right adjoint, it also preserves weak propositions. Therefore
(F_{A})_{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow(X^{\mathrm{I}})_{\mathrm{I}}
is a weak proposition, but then so is its pullback along the unit
X\rightarrow(X^{\mathrm{I}})_{\mathrm{I}}
, which is
\mathsf{Fib}_{i}(A)\rightarrow X
, the classifier for
\delta
-biased fibration structures. The same reasoning shows that
\mathsf{Fib}(A)=\rho^{*}\Pi_{i:\mathrm{I}}\mathsf{Fib}_{i}(\mathrm{I}^{*}A)
(as in (
130
)) is also a weak proposition.
∎

In light of Lemma
95
we shall assume as a final axiom on cofibrations:

(C8)

The pathobject functor preserves cofibrations: thus
c:A\rightarrowtail B
implies
c^{\mathrm{I}}:A^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrowtail B^{\mathrm{I}}
.

Now, by Propositions
88
and
91
we have universal small
\delta
-biased and unbiased fibrations, the former in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
, the latter in
\mathsf{cSet}
. The following remarks apply to both, which we refer to neutrally as
\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}
. The base object
\mathcal{U}
is (the domain of) the classifying type
\mathsf{Fib}(\dot{\mathcal{V}})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
, where
\dot{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
is the universal small family. Type theoretically, this object can be written as

\mathcal{U}=\Sigma_{E:\mathcal{V}}\mathsf{Fib}(E)\,,

which comes with the canonical projection

\mathcal{U}=\Sigma_{E:\mathcal{V}}\mathsf{Fib}(E)\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}\,.

In these terms, a fibration
E\twoheadrightarrow X
is a pair
\langle E,e\rangle
, consisting of the underlying family
E\rightarrow X
, equipped with a fibration structure
e:\mathsf{Fib}(E)
.
Lemma
95
then allows us to establish the following, which was first isolated in
[
Shu15
]
(as condition (2’), also see
[
GSS22
]
). It holds for both biased and unbiased fibrations, and will be used in the sequel to “correct” the fibration structure on certain maps.

Lemma 96

(Realignment for fibrations)
.

Given a fibration
F\twoheadrightarrow X
and a cofibration
c:C\rightarrowtail X
, let
f_{c}:C\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
classify the pullback
c^{*}F\twoheadrightarrow C
. Then there is a classifying map
f:X\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
for
F
with
f\circ c=f_{c}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{*}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(136)

Proof.

First, let
|f_{c}|:C\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
be the composite of
f_{c}:C\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
with the canonical projection
\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
, thus classifying the underlying family
c^{*}F\rightarrow C
. Next, let
f_{0}:X\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
classify the underlying family
F\rightarrow X
. We may assume that
f_{0}\circ c=|f_{c}|
by realignment for families, Proposition
81
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{*}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{\mathcal{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

(137)

Since
F\twoheadrightarrow X
is a fibration, there is a lift
f_{1}:X\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
of
f_{0}
classifying the fibration structure.
We thus have the following commutative diagram in the base of (
137
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|f_{c}|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

(138)

Now pull
\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
back against itself and rearrange the previous data to give (the solid part of) the following, which also commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}\times_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle f_{1}c,f_{c}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

(139)

Since
\mathcal{U}=\mathsf{Fib}(\dot{\mathcal{V}})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
is a weak proposition by Lemma
95
and (C8), the projection
\pi_{1}:\mathcal{U}\times_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{U}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}
is a trivial fibration, so there is a diagonal filler
f_{2}:X\rightarrow\mathcal{U}\times_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{U}
as indicated. Taking
f:=\pi_{2}\circ f_{2}:X\rightarrow\mathcal{U}\times_{\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
gives another classifying map for the fibration structure on
F\rightarrow X
, for which
f\circ c=f_{c}
as required.
∎

7.
The equivalence extension property

The equivalence extension property is closely related to the
univalence
of the universal fibration
\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}
constructed in Section
6
(see
[
Shu15
]
). It will be used in Section
8
to show that the base object
\mathcal{U}
is fibrant. The proof of the equivalence extension property given here is a reformulation of a type-theoretic argument due to Coquand
[
CCHM18
]
, which in turn is a modification of the original argument of Voevodsky
[
KL21
]
. See
[
Sat17
]
for another reformulation.

The sliced premodel structure

We begin by recalling some basic facts and making some simple observations that are well-known in general model categories, but need to be checked again here, because we do not yet have a full model structure. The reader is reminded that the word “fibration” unqualified always refers to
unbiased
fibrations as in Definition
25
. First, for any object
Z\in\mathsf{cSet}
, the slice category
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
inherits the premodel structure of Proposition
43
from
\mathsf{cSet}
via the forgetful functor

Z_{!}:\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}\longrightarrow\mathsf{cSet}\,.

In more detail:

Definition 97
.

A map
f:X\rightarrow Y
over
Z
is a
(trivial) cofibration or (trivial) fibration
over
Z
just if it is one in
\mathsf{cSet}
after forgetting the
Z
-indexing via
Z_{!}:\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}
. This will be called the (
relative
or)
sliced premodel structure
on
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
. Accordingly, a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
over
Z
will be called a
weak equivalence
over
Z
just if it factors over
Z
as a trivial fibration over
Z
after a trivial cofibration over
Z
, which therefore holds just if it is a weak equivalence in
\mathsf{cSet}
after forgetting the
Z
-indexing.

That the specification in Definition
97
actually does determine a premodel structure is a consequence of Proposition
43
, and the well-known fact that (pre-)model structures are stable under slicing in this way
[
Hir03
]
. In more detail:

Lemma 98
.

A map
f:X\rightarrow Y
over
Z
is a fibration (respectively, a trivial fibration) over
Z
if, and only if, it lifts on the right in the slice category
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
against all trivial cofibrations (respectively, cofibrations) over
Z
.

Proof.

Let
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{Y}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Z
, regarded as a map in the slice category over
Z
, with
p_{X}=p_{Y}\circ f:X\rightarrow Z
. Then by definition
f
is a fibration in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
just if
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a fibration in the total category
\mathsf{cSet}
, which holds just if
f
lifts on the right against all trivial cofibrations
t:A\rightarrowtail B
in
\mathsf{cSet}
. But every lifting problem of the form
t\pitchfork f
in
\mathsf{cSet}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}

gives rise to a corresponding one over
Z
, just by composing everything with
p_{Y}:Y\rightarrow Z
. Moreover, the evident resulting map
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t}}{{\rightarrowtail}}B\rightarrow Z
is then a trivial cofibration over
Z
, and every such lifting problem for
f
over
Z
arises in this way. Finally, the diagonal fillers for the resulting lifting problem in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
are exactly the diagonal fillers for the original one in
\mathsf{cSet}
. Thus the map
f
over
Z
is a fibration over
Z
just in case it lifts on the right over
Z
against all trivial cofibrations over
Z
, as claimed. The case of trivial fibrations and cofibrations is exactly analogous.
∎

Lemma 99
.

A map
f:X\rightarrow Y
over
Z
is a cofibration (respectively, a trivial cofibration) over
Z
if, and only if, it lifts on the left in the slice category
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
against all trivial fibrations (respectively, fibrations) over
Z
.

Proof.

Let
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{Y}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Z
, regarded as a map in the slice category over
Z
, with
p_{X}=p_{Y}\circ f:X\rightarrow Z
. Then by definition
f
is a cofibration in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
just if
Z_{!}f:Z_{!}X\rightarrow Z_{!}Y
is a cofibration in the total category
\mathsf{cSet}
, which holds just if
Z_{!}f
lifts on the left against all trivial fibrations
t:E\twoheadrightarrow F
in
\mathsf{cSet}
.
But every lifting problem of the form
Z_{!}f\pitchfork t
in
\mathsf{cSet}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}

gives rise to a corresponding one over
Z
of the form
f\pitchfork Z^{*}t
, by pulling
t
back along
Z\rightarrow 1
. Moreover, since trivial fibrations are stable under pullback in
\mathsf{cSet}
, the pullback
Z^{*}t
is a trivial fibration, and so
Z^{*}t
is a trivial fibration over
Z
. Thus
f
is a cofibration in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
if and only if
f\pitchfork Z^{*}t
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
for all trivial fibrations
t:E\twoheadrightarrow F
in
\mathsf{cSet}
.

Now observe that for any map
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{B}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Z
over
Z
, with
p_{A}=p_{B}\circ g
, the following unit square is a pullback, as indicated below,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{*}Z_{!}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{*}Z_{!}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{*}Z_{!}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\times g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{B}}

(140)

because the graph
\eta_{A}=\langle p_{A},1_{Z}\rangle:A\rightarrow Z\times A
is a pullback of
\Delta_{A}=\langle 1_{Z},1_{Z}\rangle:Z\rightarrow Z\times Z
along
1_{Z}\times p_{A}:Z\times A\rightarrow Z\times Z
, and similarly for
\eta_{B}
. Thus in particular, every trivial fibration
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{B}}}{{\longrightarrow}}Z
over
Z
is a pullback over
Z
of one of the form
Z^{*}g:Z^{*}A\twoheadrightarrow Z^{*}B
for a trivial fibration
g:A\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathsf{cSet}
. Therefore
f
is a cofibration in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
if and only if
f\pitchfork g
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
for all trivial fibrations
g:A\twoheadrightarrow B
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
, as claimed.
The case of trivial cofibrations and fibrations is exactly analogous.
∎

Since factoring a map in the slice category is evidently given simply by factoring it after forgetting the indexing, we now have:

Proposition 100
.

The specification in Definition
97
determines a premodel structure on
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
for any object
Z\in\mathsf{cSet}
.

The reader is warned that when
Z=\mathrm{I}
there is a possibility of confusion with the
\delta
-biased fibrations in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\mathrm{I}}
, which do not in general agree with the
\mathrm{I}
-sliced (unbiased) fibrations.

In order to verify the axioms (C1)-(C8) for cofibrations, let
Z^{*}1\rightrightarrows Z^{*}\mathrm{I}
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
be the result of pulling the interval
1\rightrightarrows\mathrm{I}
back along
Z\rightarrow 1
, to obtain a bipointed object in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
that we shall write as,

\delta_{0},\delta_{1}:1_{Z}\rightrightarrows\mathrm{I}_{Z}\,.

(141)

Observe that
1_{Z}+1_{Z}\cong Z^{*}1+Z^{*}1\rightarrowtail Z^{*}\mathrm{I}
since the pullback functor
Z^{*}:\mathsf{cSet}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
preserves (co)limits and cofibrations.

Proposition 101
.

Taking
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}:1_{Z}\rightrightarrows\mathrm{I}_{Z}
as an interval, the axioms (C1)-(C8) for cofibrations are satisfied in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}

Proof.

(sketch)
The (relative) cofibration classifier in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
is the pullback
Z^{*}t:Z^{*}1\rightarrowtail Z^{*}\Phi
, which we shall write as

t_{Z}:1_{Z}\rightarrowtail\Phi_{Z}\,.

(142)

For axiom (C8), observe that for a map
c:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
, the exponential
c^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}:A^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}\rightarrow A^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
fits into a unit pullback square of the form (
140
),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{*}Z_{!}(A^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{*}\big(Z_{!}(A)^{\mathrm{I}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{*}Z_{!}(B^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{*}\big(Z_{!}(B)^{\mathrm{I}}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{*}Z_{!}(c^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{*}\big(Z_{!}(c)^{\mathrm{I}}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

(143)

So if
c
is a cofibration, so is
c^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}
.
The other axioms are routine.
∎

Lemma 102
.

For any cubical set
Z
, we have the following relative versions of the pushout-product and pullback-hom conditions involving the interval in the slice category
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
.

(1)

If
c:A\rightarrowtail B
is a cofibration in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
, then the pushout-product formed in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
with
\delta_{0}:1_{Z}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}_{Z}
, written

c\otimes_{Z}\delta_{0}:B+_{A}(A\times_{Z}\mathrm{I}_{Z})\longrightarrow B\times_{Z}\mathrm{I}_{Z}\,,

is a trivial cofibration (and similarly for
\delta_{1}:1_{Z}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}_{Z}
).

(2)

If
f:X\twoheadrightarrow Y
is a fibration in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
, then the pullback-hom formed in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
with
\delta_{0}:1_{Z}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}_{Z}
, written

\delta_{0}\Rightarrow_{Z}f:X^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}\longrightarrow Y^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}\times_{Z}X\,,

is a trivial fibration (and similarly for
\delta_{1}:1_{Z}\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}_{Z}
).

Proof.

For (1), the pushout-product
c\otimes_{Z}\delta_{0}:D\rightarrow B\times_{Z}\mathrm{I}_{Z}
over
Z
is equal to the (non-relative) pushout-product
c\otimes\delta_{0}:D\rightarrow B\times\mathrm{I}
, because
Z^{*}\delta_{0}:Z^{*}1\rightarrow Z^{*}\mathrm{I}
is constant over
Z
, so

B\times_{Z}\mathrm{I}_{Z}\cong B\times\mathrm{I}\,,

and similarly for
A
(and pushouts in the slice are created by the forgetful functor
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}
). Thus, briefly,

c\otimes_{Z}Z^{*}\delta_{0}=c\otimes\delta_{0}\,,

which is indeed a trivial cofibration.

(2) follows from (1) and lemma
99
, together with the usual adjunction between
\otimes_{Z}
and
\Rightarrow_{Z}
.
∎

In order to apply the results on weak equivalences from Section
4
in arbitrary slice categories
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
we shall also require the notions of homotopy equivalence over
Z
and weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
. We first use the relative interval
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}:1_{Z}\rightrightarrows\mathrm{I}_{Z}
(
141
) to define homotopy between maps over
Z
in the expected way, namely:

Definition 103
.

For any object
Z
and maps
f,g:X\rightrightarrows Y
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
, a
homotopy over
Z
, written

\vartheta:f\sim_{Z}g\,,

is a map over
Z
,

\vartheta:\mathrm{I}_{Z}\times_{Z}{X}\longrightarrow Y,

such that
\vartheta\circ\iota_{0}=f
and
\vartheta\circ\iota_{1}=g
,

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}_{Z}\times_{Z}\!{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{X,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y}

(144)

where, as usual,
\iota_{0},\iota_{1}
are the canonical inclusions into the ends of the cylinder,

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 37.88885pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-37.88885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\iota_{\epsilon}:X\cong 1_{Z}\times_{Z}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.83376pt\raise 5.93333pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{\epsilon}\times_{Z}X}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.88885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 61.88885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 91.88885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{I}_{Z}\times_{Z}X\,,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\epsilon=0,1.

Lemma 104
.

For any object
Z
and maps
f,g:X\rightrightarrows Y
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
, a homotopy over
Z
determines a homotopy of the underlying maps by applying the functor
Z_{!}:\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}
that forgets the
Z
-indexing,

\vartheta:f\sim_{Z}g\quad\ \mapsto\quad Z_{!}\vartheta:Z_{!}f\sim Z_{!}g\,.

Proof.

Consider the following diagram depicting a homotopy
\vartheta:f\sim_{Z}g
over
Z
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{I}\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{I}_{Z}\times_{Z}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}

Since the lower left two squares are pullbacks, we have
\mathrm{I}_{Z}\times_{Z}X\cong\mathrm{I}\times X
. So applying
Z_{!}
to
\vartheta
results in a homotopy
Z_{!}\vartheta:Z_{!}f\sim Z_{!}g
.

Note that an arbitrary homotopy
\varphi:f\sim g
will not result in one over
Z
, however, unless
\varphi
commutes with the indexing maps to
Z
.
∎

Proposition 105
.

For any object
Z
, the relation of homotopy over
Z
between maps
f,g:X\rightrightarrows Y
over
Z
is preserved by pre- and post-composition. If
X\twoheadrightarrow Z
and
Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
are both fibrations, then the relation
f\sim_{Z}g
of maps between them is an equivalence relation.

The proof is essentially the same as the corresponding one for homotopy over
1
, Proposition
45
, with the exception that both
X
and
Y
are required to be fibrant objects over
Z
, so that the exponential
Y^{X}
over
Z
is also a fibration
Y^{Z}\twoheadrightarrow Z
(by Corollary
74
).

Next we define a
connected components
functor on the full subcategory
\mathsf{Fib}_{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
of fibrations over
Z
,

(\pi_{0})_{Z}:\mathsf{Fib}_{Z}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}\,,

by taking the global sections of a fibration
F\twoheadrightarrow Z
, modulo the relation
\sim_{Z}
of homotopy over
Z
. In more detail, for
F\twoheadrightarrow Z
in
\mathsf{Fib}_{Z}
let
(\pi_{0})_{Z}(F)
be the coequalizer,

\mathrm{Hom}_{Z}(\mathrm{I}_{Z},F)\rightrightarrows\mathrm{Hom}_{Z}(1_{Z},F)\rightarrow(\pi_{0})_{Z}(F)\,,

(145)

where the two maps are given by precomposition with the interval
1_{Z}\rightrightarrows\mathrm{I}_{Z}
over
Z
, and the
\mathrm{Hom}
-sets are those in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
.

For fibrations
X\twoheadrightarrow Z
and
F\twoheadrightarrow Z
we then again have

(\pi_{0})_{Z}(F^{X})=\mathrm{Hom}_{Z}(X,F)/\!\!\sim_{Z}\,,

so
(\pi_{0})_{Z}(F^{X})
is the set
[X,F]_{Z}
of
Z
-homotopy equivalence classes of maps
X\rightarrow F
over
Z
.
For maps over a base object
Z
, we can then define the notions of homotopy equivalence over
Z
and, between fibrations, weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
as before (cf. Section
4
):

Definition 106
.

Let
Z
be any object in
\mathsf{cSet}
, and let
X\rightarrow Z
and
Y\rightarrow Z
be regarded as objects over
Z
.

(1)

A map
f:X\rightarrow Y
over
Z
is a
homotopy equivalence over
Z

if there is a map
g:Y\rightarrow X
over
Z
and two homotopies over
Z
,

\vartheta:g\circ f\sim_{Z}1_{X},\qquad\varphi:f\circ g\sim_{Z}1_{Y}\,.

(2)

For
X\twoheadrightarrow Z
and
Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
fibrations, a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
over
Z
is a
weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
if for every fibration
F\twoheadrightarrow Z
, the precomposition map over
Z
,

F^{f}:F^{Y}\rightarrow F^{X}\,,

is bijective on connected components,

(\pi_{0})_{Z}(F^{f}):(\pi_{0})_{Z}(F^{Y})\cong(\pi_{0})_{Z}(F^{X})\,,

where the indicated exponentials are taken in the slice category.

The proof of the following is analogous to that of the corresponding facts for the case
Z=1
(Lemmas
47
and
53
).

Lemma 107
.

The homotopy equivalences over any object
Z
satisfy the 3-for-2 condition, as do the weak homotopy equivalences over
Z
.

Proposition 108
.

For any object
Z
and fibrations
X\twoheadrightarrow Z
and
Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
, the following conditions are equivalent for any map
f:X\rightarrow Y
over
Z
.

(1)

f:X\rightarrow Y
is a weak equivalence over
Z
,

(2)

f:X\rightarrow Y
is a homotopy equivalence over
Z
,

(3)

f:X\rightarrow Y
is a weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
,

Proof.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a weak equivalence. Factor
f=tf\circ tc
with a trivial cofibration
tc:X\rightarrowtail F
followed by a trivial fibration
tf:F\twoheadrightarrow Y
, both of which are then also over
Z
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tc}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

The proof of Proposition
58

(1\!\Rightarrow\!2)
now applies over
Z
,
mutatis mutandis
, to show that
tc:X\rightarrowtail F
is a homotopy equivalence over
Z
. Similarly, the proof of Lemma
48
also works over
Z
to show that
tf:F\twoheadrightarrow Y
is a homotopy equivalence over
Z
. Thus
f=tf\circ tc
is a homotopy equivalence over
Z
.

Any homotopy equivalence over
Z
is clearly a weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
, by the same proof as for Lemma
52
(using the fact that
X\twoheadrightarrow Z
and
Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
in order to form the required exponentials).

If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a weak homotopy equivalence, then factor it as
f=tf\circ c
with a cofibration
c:X\rightarrowtail F
followed by a trivial fibration
tf:F\twoheadrightarrow Y
, both of which are also over
Z
. We thus just need to show that
c:X\rightarrowtail F
is a trivial cofibration. As in the first step,
tf:F\twoheadrightarrow Y
is a homotopy equivalence over
Z
, whence a weak homotopy equivalence by the second step, and so by 3-for-2 for weak homotopy equivalences over
Z
, Lemma
107
,
c:X\rightarrowtail F
is also a weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
. Now, as in the proof of Proposition
58
, factor
c=f\circ tc
as a trivial cofibration
tc:X\rightarrowtail C
followed by a fibration
f:C\twoheadrightarrow F
, both over
Z
. By steps 1 and 2,
tc:X\rightarrowtail C
is then a weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
. By 3-for-2 for weak homotopy equivalences, Lemma
107
,
f:C\twoheadrightarrow F
is also a weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
. It remains to show that the fibration
f:C\twoheadrightarrow F
is a weak equivalence. This follows by repeating the reasoning for Lemma
57
, and the results leading up to it, over
Z
.
∎

Using Lemma
107
we now have:

Corollary 109
.

For any object
Z
, the weak equivalences between fibrations into
Z
satisfy the 3-for-2 condition.

Remark 110
.

Our immediate goal has been to show Corollary
109
, which will be used to establish the equivalence extension property. Proposition
112
below, which assumes the fibration extension property, will emphatically
not
be used in the sequel, but is included here simply to complete the study of the relative (pre)model structure.

Lemma 111
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be any map over
Z
.

(1)

If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is homotopy equivalence over
Z
, then
Z_{!}f:Z_{!}X\rightarrow Z_{!}Y
is a homotopy equivalence.

(2)

If
X\twoheadrightarrow Z
and
Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
are fibrations and
f:X\rightarrow Y
is weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
, then
Z_{!}f:Z_{!}X\rightarrow Z_{!}Y
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

(1) is immediate from the fact that
Z_{!}
preserves homotopies, Lemma
104
.
For (2), let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
between fibrations
X\twoheadrightarrow Z
and
Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
, and let
K
be any fibrant object in
\mathsf{cSet}
.
Consider the internal precomposition map,

K^{Z_{!}f}:K^{Z_{!}Y}\rightarrow K^{Z_{!}X}\,,

which we would like to show is a bijection under
\pi_{0}:\mathsf{cSet}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
.
Since
K\twoheadrightarrow 1
is a fibration, so is its pullback
Z^{*}K\twoheadrightarrow Z
. Therefore, since
f:X\rightarrow Y
is weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
,
the precomposition map over
Z
,

(Z^{*}K)^{f}:(Z^{*}K)^{Y}\rightarrow(Z^{*}K)^{X}\,,

is bijective on connected components,

(\pi_{0})_{Z}\big((Z^{*}K)^{f}\big):(\pi_{0})_{Z}\big((Z^{*}K)^{Y}\big)\cong(\pi_{0})_{Z}\big((Z^{*}K)^{X}\big)\,.

But now observe that in the coequalizer (
145
) that defines
(\pi_{0})_{Z}\big((Z^{*}K)^{X}\big)
, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{Z}(1_{Z},(Z^{*}K)^{X})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{Z}(X,(Z^{*}K))\\
\cong\mathrm{Hom}(Z_{!}X,K)\cong\mathrm{Hom}(1,K^{Z_{!}X})\,,\end{split}

and similarly

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{Z}(\mathrm{I}_{Z},(Z^{*}K)^{X})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{Z}(Z^{*}\mathrm{I}\times X,Z^{*}K)\\
\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{Z}(\mathrm{I}\times Z_{!}X,K)\cong\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{I},K^{Z_{!}X})\,.\end{split}

Thus
(\pi_{0})_{Z}((Z^{*}K)^{X})\cong(\pi_{0})(K^{Z_{!}X})
, and the same is true with
Y
in place of
X
. So
K^{Z_{!}f}:K^{Z_{!}Y}\rightarrow K^{Z_{!}X}
is also bijective on connected components.
∎

Proposition 112
.

Let
Z
be any object in
\mathsf{cSet}
and
X\twoheadrightarrow Z
and
Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
fibrations. For any map
f:X\rightarrow Y
over
Z
, the following conditions are equivalent,
assuming the fibration extension property, Corollary
123
.

(1)

f:X\rightarrow Y
is a weak equivalence over
Z
.

(2)

f:X\rightarrow Y
is a homotopy equivalence over
Z
.

(3)

f:X\rightarrow Y
is a weak homotopy equivalence over
Z
.

(4)

Z_{!}f:Z_{!}X\rightarrow Z_{!}Y
is a weak equivalence.

(5)

Z_{!}f:Z_{!}X\rightarrow Z_{!}Y
is a homotopy equivalence in
\mathsf{cSet}
.

(6)

Z_{!}f:Z_{!}X\rightarrow Z_{!}Y
is a weak homotopy equivalence in
\mathsf{cSet}
.

Proof.

In Proposition
108
we showed the implications
1\Leftrightarrow 2\Leftrightarrow 3
.
We also have
1\Leftrightarrow 4
by definition, and
4\Rightarrow 6
and
5\Rightarrow 6
by Lemmas
55
and
52
. Moreover, by Lemma
111
we have
2\Rightarrow 5
and
3\Rightarrow 6
.
Thus all 6 conditions will be equivalent once we have
6\Rightarrow 4
, which follows from Proposition
66
and the fibration extension property, Corollary
123
.
∎

Pathobject factorizations.

For any map
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathsf{cSet}
, recall the
pathobject factorization

f=t\circ s
indicated below.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{P_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{f}}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}^{*}f}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{Y^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y}

(146)

Here
p_{0},p_{1}
are the evaluations
Y^{\delta_{0}},Y^{\delta_{1}}:Y^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow Y
at the endpoints
\delta_{0},\delta_{1}:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
, and let
r:=Y^{!}
for
!:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow 1
, so that
p_{0}r=p_{1}r=1_{Y}
. Then let
p_{f}:=f^{*}p_{0}:P_{f}\rightarrow Y
, the pullback of
p_{0}
along
f
, and
s:=f^{*}r:X\rightarrow P_{f}
(as a map over
X
). Finally, let
t:=p_{1}\circ p_{0}^{*}f:P_{f}\rightarrow Y
be the indicated horizontal composite.

We then have the following facts:

(1)

The retraction
p_{0}\circ r=1_{Y}
pulls back along
f
to a retraction
{p_{f}}\circ{s}=1_{X}
.

(2)

If
Y
is a fibrant object, then
p_{0},p_{1}:Y^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow Y
are both trivial fibrations, by Proposition
32
.

(3)

If
X
and
Y
are both fibrant then
t=p_{1}\circ p_{0}^{*}f:P_{f}\rightarrow Y
is a fibration. This can be seen by factoring the maps
p_{0},p_{1}:Y^{\mathrm{I}}\rightrightarrows Y
through the product projections as

\pi_{0}\circ p,\ \pi_{1}\circ p:Y^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow Y\times Y\rightrightarrows Y

where
p=(p_{0},p_{1})
, and then interpolating the pullback
(f,1_{Y}):X\times Y\rightarrow Y\times Y
into (
146
) as indicated below.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces P_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}p}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}^{*}f}
\textstyle{Y^{\mathrm{I}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces X\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{(f,1_{Y})}
\textstyle{Y\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y}

(147)

The second factor
t=p_{1}\circ p_{0}^{*}f:P_{f}\rightarrow Y
now appears also as
\pi_{1}\circ(f,1_{Y})\circ f^{*}p
, which is equal to the pullback
f^{*}p:P_{f}\rightarrow X\times Y
followed by the second projection
\pi^{\prime}_{1}:X\times Y\rightarrow Y
(which is not a pullback). But if
Y
is fibrant, then
p:Y^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow Y\times Y
is a fibration by the
{\otimes}\dashv{\Rightarrow}
adjunction, since
p=\partial\Rightarrow Y
(this is just as in Proposition
32
, but with the cofibration
\partial:1+1\rightarrowtail\mathrm{I}
in place of the trivial cofibration
\delta_{\epsilon}:1\rightarrow\mathrm{I}
).
Therefore the pullback
f^{*}p
is also a fibration. And if
X
is fibrant, then the second projection
\pi^{\prime}_{1}:X\times Y\rightarrow Y
is a fibration. Thus in this case,
t=\pi^{\prime}_{1}\circ f^{*}p:P_{f}\rightarrow Y
is a fibration, as claimed.

Summarizing (1)-(3):

Lemma 113
.

For any map
f:X\rightarrow Y

there is a factorization
f=t\circ s
,

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces P_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{f}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{Y}

(148)

in which

(1)

s
is a section of a map
p_{f}:P_{f}\rightarrow X
,

(2)

if
Y
is fibrant, then
p_{f}
is a trivial fibration,

(3)

if both
X
and
Y
are fibrant, then
t
is a fibration.

Note that the retraction
p_{f}:P_{f}\rightarrow X
of
s
is not over
Y
.

Next, if
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a map over any base object
Z
in
\mathsf{cSet}
, we can use the same factorization
f=t\circ s
to get a factorization in the slice category over
Z
,

\textstyle{P_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{Y}}
\textstyle{Z}

(149)

with
p_{Y}\circ t:P_{f}\rightarrow Z
; however, the maps
s,t
will no longer have the properties stated in Lemma
113
, because
e.g.

p_{0}:Y^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow Y
need not be a trivial fibration, even when
p_{Y}:Y\rightarrow Z
is a fibration, since the object
Y
need not be fibrant if the base
Z
is not fibrant.

To remedy this, we can instead build a
fiberwise pathobject factorization
by using the relative pathobject
Y^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}\rightarrow Z
, where the indicated exponential is taken in the slice over
Z
, and the interval object
\mathrm{I}_{Z}
occurring in the exponent is the relative one from (
141
),
i.e.
the result of pulling the interval
\mathrm{I}
back from
\mathsf{cSet}
along
Z\rightarrow 1
. The pathspace factorization is then constructed as in (
146
), but now in the slice
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
, using the pulled back interval
1_{Z}\rightrightarrows\mathrm{I}_{Z}
. Moreover, the resulting factorization
f=t\circ s:X\rightarrow P_{f}\rightarrow Y
is then stable under pullback along any map
g:Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Z
, in the sense that
g^{*}(Y^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}})\cong g^{*}(Y)^{\mathrm{I}_{Z^{\prime}}}
and so
g^{*}P_{f}=P_{g^{*}f}
, where
g^{*}f:g^{*}X\rightarrow g^{*}Y
, and similarly for the factors
g^{*}s
and
g^{*}t
.

In more detail, let us review the foregoing steps in the relative case, with reference to the following diagram.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{P_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{f}}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}^{*}f}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{Y^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{p_{Y}}
\textstyle{Z}

(150)

(1)

The exponential of
p_{Y}:Y\rightarrow Z
, taken in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{\!Z}
, by the
constant
maps
Z^{*}\delta_{\epsilon}:Z^{*}1\rightarrow Z^{*}\mathrm{I}
, which we write as
\delta_{\epsilon}:1_{Z}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}_{Z}
, are now maps
p_{\epsilon}:=Y^{\delta_{\epsilon}}:Y^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}\rightarrow Y
over
Z
, for
\epsilon=0,1
. The retraction
p_{0}\circ r=1_{Y}
(with
r
defined accordingly) is now also over
Z
, and it still pulls back along
f
to a retraction
p_{f}\circ s=1_{X}
, also over
Z
.

(2)

If
p_{Y}:Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
is a fibration, then the maps
p_{0},p_{1}:Y^{\mathrm{I}_{Z}}\rightarrow Y
over
Z
are again trivial fibrations by Lemma
102
, since these are pullback-homs over
Z
of the form
\delta_{\epsilon}\Rightarrow_{Z}Y
.

(3)

If
X\twoheadrightarrow Z
and
Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
are both fibrations, then for the same reason
t=p_{1}\circ p_{0}^{*}f:P_{f}\rightarrow Y
is a fibration.

Again, summarizing (1)-(3) in the relative case:

Lemma 114
.

For any map
f:X\rightarrow Y
over any base
Z\in\mathsf{cSet}
,
there is a stable factorization
f=t\circ s
over
Z
,

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces P_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{f}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces Y}

(151)

in which

(1)

s:X\rightarrow P_{f}
is a section of a map
p_{f}:P_{f}\rightarrow X
over
Z
,

(2)

if
Y\rightarrow Z
is a fibration, then
p_{f}:P_{f}\rightarrow X
is a trivial fibration,

(3)

if both
X\rightarrow Z
and
Y\rightarrow Z
are fibrations, then
t:P_{f}\rightarrow Y
is a fibration.

Note that the retraction
p_{f}:P_{f}\rightarrow X
of
s
is not over
Y
.

The following fact concerning just the cofibration weak factorization system will also be needed.

Lemma 115
.

Let
p:E\twoheadrightarrow B
be a trivial fibration and
c:C\rightarrowtail B
a cofibration. Then the unit
\eta:E\rightarrow c_{*}c^{*}E
over
B
of the base change along
c
,

c^{*}\dashv c_{*}:\mathsf{cSet}/_{C}\longrightarrow\mathsf{cSet}/_{B}

is also a trivial fibration.

Proof.

Regarding
c:C\rightarrowtail B
as a subobject
C\rightarrowtail 1_{B}
in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{B}
, the unit map
\eta:E\rightarrow c_{*}c^{*}E=E^{C}
is the pullback-hom
c\!\Rightarrow_{B}\!p
in the slice category over
B
, as shown below.

\textstyle{E^{1_{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{1_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{c\,\Rightarrow_{B}\,{p}}
\scriptstyle{E^{c}}
\textstyle{B^{\mathrm{I}}\times_{B^{C}}E^{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{C}}
\textstyle{B^{1_{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B^{c}}
\textstyle{B^{C}}

(152)

We use the fact that in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{B}
we have
B^{c}:B^{1_{B}}\cong 1_{B}\cong B^{C}
and so

(c\Rightarrow_{B}p)=E^{c}:E\longrightarrow E^{C}\,,

which is indeed
\eta:E\rightarrow c_{*}c^{*}E=E^{C}
.

Now for any cofibration
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrowtail}}X\rightarrow B
over
B
, by Lemma
21
we have an equivalence of diagonal filling conditions in
\mathsf{cSet}/_{B}
,

a\pitchfork(c\!\Rightarrow_{B}\!p)\quad\text{iff}\quad(a\!\otimes_{B}\!c)\pitchfork p.

But since
c:C\rightarrowtail B
is a cofibration,
a\otimes_{B}c
is also a cofibration, since
a:A\rightarrowtail X
is one, and by axiom (C6), cofibrations are closed under pushout-products. Thus
(a\otimes_{B}c)\pitchfork p
indeed holds, since
p
is a trivial fibration.
∎

Proposition 116

(Equivalence extension property)
.

Weak equivalences extended along cofibrations in the following sense: given a cofibration
c:C^{\prime}\rightarrowtail C
and fibrations
A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow C^{\prime}
and
B\twoheadrightarrow C
, and a weak equivalence
w^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\simeq c^{*}B
over
C^{\prime}
,

\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{w^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{w}
\textstyle{c^{*}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{C}

(153)

there is a fibration
A\twoheadrightarrow C
and a weak equivalence
w:A\simeq B
over
C
that pulls back along
c:C^{\prime}\rightarrowtail C
to
w^{\prime}
, so
c^{*}w=w^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Call the given fibration
q:B\rightarrow C
and let
b:=q^{*}c:c^{*}B\rightarrow B
be the indicated pullback, which is thus also a cofibration. Let
w:=b_{*}w^{\prime}:A\rightarrow B
be the pushforward of
w^{\prime}
along
b
. Composing
w
with
q
gives the map
p:=q\circ w:A\rightarrow C
. Since
b
is monic, we indeed have
b^{*}w=w^{\prime}
, thus filling in all the dotted arrows in (
153
). Note moreover that
c^{*}w=b^{*}w=w^{\prime}
, as required. It remains to show that
p:A\rightarrow C
is a fibration and
w:A\rightarrow B
is a weak equivalence.

\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{w^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces P_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{w}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{c^{*}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{C}

(154)

Let us name
p^{\prime}:=c^{*}p:A^{\prime}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}:=c^{*}B
and
q^{\prime}:=c^{*}q
.
Now let
w=t\circ s
be the (relative) pathspace factorization (
150
) of

, as a map over
C
. Since
q:B\rightarrow C
is a fibration, by Lemma
114
, we know that
s:A\rightarrow P_{w}
has a retraction
p_{w}:P_{w}\rightarrow A
over

which is a trivial fibration.

The pathspace factorization
w=t\circ s:A\rightarrow P_{w}\rightarrow B
is stable under pullback along
c
, providing a pathspace factorization of
c^{*}w=w^{\prime}=t^{\prime}\circ s^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow P_{w^{\prime}}\rightarrow B^{\prime}
over
C^{\prime}
. Since both
p^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
are fibrations, the retraction
p_{w^{\prime}}:P_{w^{\prime}}\rightarrow A^{\prime}
is a trivial fibration, and now
t^{\prime}:P_{w^{\prime}}\rightarrow B^{\prime}
is a fibration.

\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{w^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{s^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces P_{w^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}}

(155)

Thus the composite
q^{\prime}\circ t^{\prime}:P_{w^{\prime}}\rightarrow B^{\prime}\rightarrow C^{\prime}
is a fibration and therefore, by the retraction over
C^{\prime}
with the trivial fibration
p_{w^{\prime}}
, we have that

is a weak equivalence, by 3-for-2 for weak equivalences between fibrations, Corollary
109
. For the same reason,
t^{\prime}
is then a weak equivalence, and therefore a trivial fibration.

Since

is a trivial fibration, its pushforward

along
b
is also one, by Corollary
18
. Moreover,

admits a unit

(over

).

\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{w^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces P_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{b_{*}b^{*}P_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{C}

(156)

We now

that

is a trivial fibration. Given that, the composite
t=b_{*}b^{*}t\circ\eta
is also a trivial fibration, whence

is a fibration, and so its retract
p:A\rightarrow C
is a fibration. Moreover, since

is a section of the trivial fibration
p_{w}:P_{w}\rightarrow A
between fibrations, again by Corollary
109
it is also a weak equivalence. Thus

is a weak equivalence, and we are finished.

To prove the remaining claim that
\eta:P_{w}\rightarrow b_{*}b^{*}P_{w}
is a trivial fibration, we shall use lemma
115
. It does not apply directly, however, since

is not yet known to be a trivial fibration. Instead, we show that

is a pullback of the corresponding unit at the trivial fibration

.

Consider the following cube (viewed with

at the front).

\textstyle{P_{w^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{(p^{\prime}_{0})^{*}w^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{p_{0}}

\textstyle{B}

The right hand face is a pullback by definition, and the remainder results from pulling the entire right face back along

, by the stability of the pathspace factorization, Lemma

. Thus all faces in the cube (

)
are pullbacks. The base is also a pushforward,
b_{*}w^{\prime}=w
, again by definition. Thus the top face is also a pushforward,

. Indeed, since the front face is a pullback, the Beck-Chevalley condition applies, and so we have
\overline{b}_{*}(p^{\prime}_{0})^{*}(w^{\prime})=p_{0}^{*}\,b_{*}(w^{\prime})=p_{0}^{*}w
.

Now consider the following, in which the top square remains the same as in (

The horizontal direction is still pullback along

; let us rename

and

and

to make this clear. We then add the pushforward along

on the right, in order to obtain the two units

.

\textstyle{b^{*}P_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{B}

(159)

By the usual calculation of pushforwards in slice categories,
\overline{b}_{*}\cong\eta_{p_{1}}^{*}\circ b_{*}
, and so for
b^{*}u
we have
\overline{b}_{*}b^{*}u=\eta_{p_{1}}^{*}b_{*}b^{*}u
. But as we just determined in (
157
) the top left square is already a pushforward, and therefore
u=\eta_{p_{1}}^{*}b_{*}b^{*}u
, so the top right naturality square is a pullback.

To finish the proof as planned,
p_{1}:B^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow B
is a trivial fibration because
q:B\rightarrow C
is a fibration, and
b:B^{\prime}\rightarrowtail B
is a cofibration because it is a pullback of
c:C^{\prime}\rightarrowtail C
. Thus by lemma
115
, we have that
\eta_{p_{1}}:B^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow b_{*}b^{*}B^{\mathrm{I}}
is a trivial fibration, and so its pullback
\eta_{t}:P_{w}\rightarrow b_{*}b^{*}P_{w}
is a trivial fibration, as claimed.
∎

Remark 117
.

Note that
p:A\rightarrow C
is small if
q:B\rightarrow C
is small.

8.
The fibration extension property

Given a universal fibration
\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}
, such as
\dot{\mathsf{Fib}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{Fib}
of Proposition
91
, the fibration extension property (Definition
63
) is closely related to the statement that the base object
\mathcal{U}
is fibrant. For Kan simplicial sets, Voevodsky proved the latter directly, using the theory of minimal fibrations
[
KL21
]
. In a more general (but still simplicial) setting, Shulman
[
Shu15
]
gives a proof using univalence, in the form of the equivalence extension property of Section
7
, but that proof also uses the 3-for-2 property for weak equivalences, which we do not yet have. For cubical sets, Coquand
[
CCHM18
]
uses the equivalence extension property to prove that
\mathcal{U}
is fibrant without assuming 3-for-2 for weak equivalences, via a neat type theoretic argument reducing box-filling to an operation of
Kan-composition
. We shall prove that
\mathcal{U}
is fibrant using the equivalence extension property, also without assuming 3-for-2 for weak equivalences, but via a different argument than that in
[
CCHM18
]
not using (type theory or) Kan composition.

Returning to the relation between the fibration extension property and the fibrancy of the base object of the universal fibration
\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}
, it is easy to see that the latter implies the former. Indeed, let
t:X\rightarrowtail X^{\prime}
be a trivial cofibration and
Y\twoheadrightarrow X
a fibration. To extend
Y
along
t
, take a classifying map
y:X\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
, so that
Y\cong y^{*}\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}
over
X
. If
\mathcal{U}
is fibrant then we can extend
y
along
t:X\rightarrowtail X^{\prime}
to get
y^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
with
y=y^{\prime}\circ t
. The pullback
Y^{\prime}=(y^{\prime})^{*}\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\twoheadrightarrow X^{\prime}
is then a (small) fibration such that
t^{*}Y^{\prime}\cong t^{*}(y^{\prime})^{*}\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\cong y^{*}\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\cong Y
over
X
.

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{\mathcal{U}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y^{\prime}}

Thus, for the record, we have:

Proposition 118
.

If the base object
\,\mathcal{U}
of the universal fibration
\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}
is fibrant, then the fibration weak factorization system has the fibration extension property.

Conversely, given the Realignment Lemma
96
, the fibration extension property also implies the fibrancy of
\mathcal{U}
:

Corollary 119
.

The fibration extension property implies that the base
\mathcal{U}
of the universal fibration
\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}
is fibrant: given any
y:X\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
and trivial cofibration
t:X\rightarrowtail X^{\prime}
, there is a map
y^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
with
y^{\prime}\circ t=y
.

Proof.

Take the pullback of
\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}
along
y:X\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
to get a (small) a fibration
Y\twoheadrightarrow X
, which extends along the (trivial) cofibration
t:X\rightarrowtail X^{\prime}
by the fibration extension property, to a (small) fibration
Y^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow X^{\prime}
with
Y\cong t^{*}Y^{\prime}
over
X
. By realignment there is a classifying map
y^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
for
Y^{\prime}
with
y^{\prime}\circ t=y
.
∎

Now let us show the following.

Proposition 120
.

The base
\mathcal{U}
of the universal fibration
\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}
in
\mathsf{cSet}
, as constructed in Section
6
, is a fibrant object.

By Corollary
27
,
\mathcal{U}
is an unbiased fibrant object if the canonical map

in the following diagram in

\textstyle{\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{eval}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{p}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{\mathrm{I}\times\mathcal{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces A_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{c}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{a}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{\langle ic\rangle}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces C\times\mathrm{I}}
\scriptstyle{c\times\mathrm{I}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{b}}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{d}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle i\rangle}
\textstyle{Z\times\mathrm{I}}

(164)

We shall construct
p_{d}:D\twoheadrightarrow Z\times\mathrm{I}
using the equivalence extension property (Proposition
116
) as follows. First apply the functor
(-)\times\mathrm{I}
to the left vertical (pullback) face of the cube in (

) to get the following, with a new pullback square on the right with the indicated fibrations.
