3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 146.09248pt\raise-75.50002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 184.42278pt\raise-75.50002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(165)

We now
claim
that there is a weak equivalence
e:A\simeq A_{c}\times\mathrm{I}
over
C\times\mathrm{I}
. From this it follows by the equivalence extension property (Proposition
116
) that there are:

(i)

a fibration
p_{d}:D\twoheadrightarrow Z\times\mathrm{I}
with
(c\times\mathrm{I})^{*}D\cong A
over
C\times\mathrm{I}
, and

(ii)

a weak equivalence
f:D\simeq B\times\mathrm{I}
over
Z\times\mathrm{I}
with
(c\times\mathrm{I})^{*}f\cong e
over
C\times\mathrm{I}
.

It then remains only to show that
B\cong\langle i\rangle^{*}D
over
Z
to complete the proof.

To obtain the claimed weak equivalence
e
, consider the following square,

\textstyle{A_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle icp_{c}\rangle}
\textstyle{A_{c}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{c}\times\mathrm{I}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{a}}
\textstyle{C\times\mathrm{I}\,,}

(166)

in which the top horizontal map is the graph of the composite,

A_{c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{c}}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\rightarrowtail}}Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{I}\,,

and the others are the evident ones from (
165
).
The square is easily seen to commute, and the top map is a trivial cofibration (by Remark
31
), because it is the graph of a map into
\mathrm{I}
. The left map is also a trivial cofibration by Frobenius (Proposition
72
), because by its definition in (
164
) it is the pullback of another such graph
\langle ic\rangle
along the fibration
p_{a}
. A simple lemma (Lemma
121
below) provides the claimed weak equivalence
e:A\simeq A_{c}\times\mathrm{I}
over
C\times\mathrm{I}
.

To see that
B\cong\langle i\rangle^{*}D
over
Z
, recall from the proof of the equivalence extension property that the map
f:D\cong B\times\mathrm{I}
is the pushforward of
e:A\simeq A_{c}\times\mathrm{I}
along the cofibration
b_{c}\times\mathrm{I}:A_{c}\times\mathrm{I}\rightarrowtail B\times\mathrm{I}
, where we are calling the evident map in (
165
)
b_{c}:A_{c}\rightarrowtail B
. Thus by construction
f=(b_{c}\times\mathrm{I})_{*}\,e
. We can then apply the Beck-Chevalley condition for the pushforward using the pullback square on the left below.

\textstyle{A_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\langle icp_{c}\rangle}
\textstyle{A_{c}\times\mathrm{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b_{c}\times\mathrm{I}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle ip_{b}\rangle}
\textstyle{B\times\mathrm{I}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{f}

(167)

The pullback of
e
along the top of the square is the identity on
A_{c}
, as can be seen by pulling back
e
as a map over
C\times\mathrm{I}
along
\langle ic\rangle:C\rightarrow C\times\mathrm{I}
. Thus the same is true up to isomorophism for the pullback of
f
along the bottom.

An application of the Realignment Lemma
96
along the trivial cofibration
c\otimes_{i}\delta
completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 121
.

Suppose the following square commutes and the indicated cofibrations are trivial.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D}

(168)

Then there is a weak equivalence
e:B\simeq C
over
D
(and under
A
).

Proof.

Use the fact that any two diagonal fillers are homotopic to get a homotopy equivalence
e:B\simeq C
filling the square.
∎

Remark 122
.

The foregoing proof of Proposition
120
, the fibrancy of the universe
\mathcal{U}
, also works,
mutatis mutandis
, for the universe of
biased
fibrations, as used in the setting of
[
CCHM18
]
.

Applying proposition
118
now yields the following.

Corollary 123

(Fibration extension property)
.

The fibration weak factorization system has the fibration extension property (definition
63
).

By Theorem
67
, finally, we have the following.

Theorem 124
.

There is a Quillen model structure
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{F})
on the category
\mathsf{cSet}
of cubical sets for which:

(1)

the cofibrations
\mathcal{C}
are any class of maps satisfying (C0)-(C8) (equivalently, the simplified axioms in Appendix A),

(2)

the fibrations
\mathcal{F}
are the maps
f:Y\rightarrow X
for which the canonical map

(f^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I},\mathsf{eval}_{Y}):Y^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I}\longrightarrow(X^{\mathrm{I}}\times\mathrm{I})\times_{X}Y

lifts on the right against
\mathcal{C}
.

(3)

the weak equivalences
\mathcal{W}
are the maps
w:X\rightarrow Y
for which the internal precomposition
K^{w}:K^{Y}\rightarrow K^{X}
is bijective on connected components for every fibrant object
K
.

Remark 125
.

We note that in terms of the universal fibration
\,\dot{\mathcal{U}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}
constructed in Section
6
the equivalence extension property Proposition
116
says that the second projection from the classifying type of equivalences
A\simeq B
between small families,

\pi_{2}:\Sigma_{A,B}\mathsf{Eq}(A,B)\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}\,,

is a trivial fibration. From this, it follows that the canonical transport map

*:\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{I}}\longrightarrow\Sigma_{A,B}\mathsf{Eq}(A,B)

(169)

is an equivalence over the base
\mathcal{U}
via
p_{2}:\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
, which is a trivial fibration because
\mathcal{U}
is fibrant by Proposition
120
. In type theory, the pathobject
\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{I}}
of course interprets the identity type
A=B
, so the equivalence (
169
) can be expressed as

(A=B)\simeq(A\simeq B)\,.

Appendix A: Axioms for Cartesian cofibrations

A system of maps satisfying the axioms (C0)-(C8) above for the cofibrations in a cartesian cubical model category will be called
cartesian cofibrations
. The axioms may be restated equivalently as follows.

(A0)

All cofibrations are monomorphisms.

(A1)

All isomorphisms are cofibrations.

(A2)

The composite of two cofibrations is a cofibration.

(A3)

Any pullback of a cofibration is a cofibration.

(A4)

The join of two cofibrant subobjects is a cofibration.

(A5)

The diagonal of the interval
\mathrm{I}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}\times\mathrm{I}
is a cofibration.

(A6)

Cofibrations are preserved by the pathobject functor
(-)^{\mathrm{I}}
.

(A7)

The category of cofibrations and Cartesian squares has a terminal object .

Example 126
.

Consider the cartesian cubical presheaves
\mathsf{c}\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}^{{\boxempty}^{\mathrm{op}}}\!=\mathcal{E}^{\mathbb{B}}
in a topos
\mathcal{E}
.
For such (internal) discrete opfibrations
(A,\,\alpha)
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}_{1}\times_{\mathbb{B}_{0}}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{dom}}

over the (internal) category
\mathbb{B}=(\mathbb{B}_{1}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{B}_{0})
of finite bipointed sets, call a subpresheaf
c:(C,\gamma)\rightarrow(A,\alpha)

locally complemented
if the underlying map
c:C\rightarrow A
over
\mathbb{B}_{0}=\mathbb{N}
is a complemented subobject in
\mathcal{E}/_{\!\mathbb{N}}
,
i.e.

C+\neg{C}\cong A
over
\mathbb{N}
. Internally, this means that

C_{n}+\neg(C_{n})\cong A_{n}\qquad\text{for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$},

(170)

which is a weaker condition than
(A,\alpha)+\neg(A,\alpha)\cong(B,\beta)
as presheaves (unless
\mathcal{E}=\mathsf{Set}
, in which case it is trivial).

Proposition 127
.

For any topos
\mathcal{E}
, the locally complemented subobjects in the category
\mathsf{c}\mathcal{E}
of cubical
\mathcal{E}
-objects satisfy the axioms for cartesian cofibrations.

Proof.

Axioms (A0)-(A4) are satisfied by the complemented subobjects in
\mathcal{E}/_{\!\mathbb{N}}
, and the forgetful functor
U:\mathsf{c}\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}/_{\!\mathbb{N}}
creates the monos, isos, composites, pullbacks, and joins in question.
For (A5), we use the fact that the equality relation on
\mathbb{N}
is decidable to infer that, for each
[n]=\{0,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},1\}
, the finite set
\{i=j\,|\,0\leq i,j\leq n+1\}
is complemented in
\mathbb{B}([1],[n])\times\mathbb{B}([1],[n])
, and so for the subpresheaf
\delta:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow\mathrm{I}\times\mathrm{I}
we indeed have,

\delta_{n}+\neg(\delta_{n})\cong\mathrm{I}_{n}\times\mathrm{I}_{n}\qquad\text{for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$}\,.

(171)

For (A6) we use the fact that the pathobject
A^{\mathrm{I}}
is a shift by one dimension, together with
(A^{\mathrm{I}})_{n}=A_{n+1}
, together with (
170
).
The cofibration classifier in (A7) is given by applying the right adjoint
U\dashv R:\mathcal{E}/_{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow\mathsf{c}\mathcal{E}
to the complemented subobject classifier
\mathbb{N}^{*}2
of
\mathcal{E}/_{\!\mathbb{N}}
.
∎

Appendix B: Cartesian cubical sets classifies intervals

Recall from Section
1
that the objects of the
Cartesian cube category

\Box
may be taken concretely to be finite, strictly bipointed sets, written

[n]=\{0,x_{1},...,x_{n},1\},

and the arrows
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
to be all bipointed maps
[m]\rightarrow[n]
(note the direction).
The category of
(Cartesian) cubical sets
is then the presheaf topos

\mathsf{cSet}=\mathsf{Set}^{\Box^{\mathrm{op}}}.

It is generated by the
n
-cubes

\mathrm{I}^{n}=\mathsf{y}[n]
, with
1=\mathrm{I}^{0}
,
\mathrm{I}=\mathsf{y}[1]
; and
\mathrm{I}^{n}\times\mathrm{I}^{m}\cong\mathrm{I}^{n+m}
by preservation of products by the Yoneda embedding
\mathsf{y}:{\Box}^{\mathrm{op}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{cSet}
. For a cubical set
X:{\Box}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
we have the usual Yoneda correspondence for the set
X_{n}
of
n
-cubes in
X
,

\{c\in X_{n}\}\ \cong\ \{c:\mathrm{I}^{n}\rightarrow X\}.

In particular,
\mathrm{I}^{n}_{m}=\boxempty([m],[n])
is the set of
m
-cubes in the
n
-cube.
2
2

2

Note that the cardinality of
\mathrm{I}^{n}_{m}
is therefore just
(m+2)^{n}
, in comparison to the
Dedekind
cubes
\boxempty_{\wedge,\vee}
used in
[
CCHM18
,
OP18
]
, for which
e.g.

\boxempty_{\wedge,\vee}([n],[1])
is the
n^{th}

Dedekind number
, the number of elements in the free distributive lattice on
n
generators, which is in general a number so large that it is unknown for values of
n>8
.

Proposition 128
.

The category
\mathsf{cSet}
of Cartesian cubical sets is the classifying topos for
intervals
: objects
\mathcal{I}
with points
i,j:1\rightrightarrows\mathcal{I}
the pullback of which is
0
:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\mathcal{I}}

Proof.

Consider the covariant presentation
\mathsf{cSet}=\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}}
where
\mathbb{B}
is the category of finite, strictly bipointed sets and bipointed maps. We can extend
\mathbb{B}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{B}_{=}
by freely adjoining coequalizers, making
\mathbb{B}_{=}
the free finite
colimit
category on a co-bipointed object. A concrete presentation of
\mathbb{B}_{=}
is the finite bipointed sets, including those with
0=1
. Let us write
(n)
for the bipointed set
\{x_{1},...,x_{n},*\}
, with
n
(non-constant) elements and a further element
0=*=1
. There is an evident coequalizer
[1]\rightrightarrows[n]\rightarrow(n)
, which (only) identifies the distinguished points, and every coqualizer in
\mathbb{B}_{=}
has either the form
[m]\rightrightarrows[n]\rightarrow[k]
or
[m]\rightrightarrows[n]\rightarrow(k)
, for a suitable choice of
k
. Note that there are no maps of the form
(m)\rightarrow[n]
, and that every map
[m]\rightarrow(n)
factors uniquely as
[m]\rightarrow(m)\rightarrow(n)
with
[m]\rightarrow(m)
the canonical coequalizer of
0
and
1
. The category
\mathbb{B}_{=}
can therefore be decomposed into two “levels”, the upper one of which is essentially
\mathbb{B}
, and the lower one consisting of just the objects
(n)
, and thus essentially the finite
pointed
sets, and for each
n
, there is the canonical coequalizer
[n]\rightarrow(n)
going from the upper level to the lower one.

\textstyle{\dots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[m]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{\dots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(m)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}

Write
u:\mathbb{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}_{=}
for the upper inclusion, which is the classifying functor of generic co-bipointed object in
\mathbb{B}_{=}
.

Now consider the induced geometric morphism:

\textstyle{\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{!}}
\scriptstyle{u_{*}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}_{=}}}
\scriptstyle{\,u^{*}}
\textstyle{u_{!}\dashv u^{*}\dashv u_{*}}

Since
u^{*}
is the restriction along
u
, the right adjoint
u_{*}
must be “prolongation by
1
”,

\displaystyle u_{*}(P)[n]

\displaystyle=P[n],

\displaystyle u_{*}(P)(n)

\displaystyle=\{*\},

with the obvious maps,

\textstyle{\dots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P[m]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P[n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{\dots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{*\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{*\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}

as is easily seen by considering maps in
\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}_{=}}
of the form

\textstyle{Q[n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{P[n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{Q(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{*\}.}

Since
u_{*}:\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}_{=}}
is evidently full and faithful, it is the inclusion part of a sheaf subtopos
\mathsf{sh}(\mathbb{B}^{\mathsf{op}}_{=},j)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}_{=}}
for a suitable Grothendieck topology
j
on
\mathbb{B}^{\mathsf{op}}_{=}
. We claim that
j
is the closed complement topology of the subobject
[0=1]\rightarrowtail 1
represented by the coequalizer
[0]\rightarrow(0)
. Indeed, in
\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}_{=}}
we have the representable functors:

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}

\displaystyle=y[1],

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=y[0],

\displaystyle[0=1]

\displaystyle=y(0)

fitting into an equalizer
[0=1]\rightarrow 1\rightrightarrows\mathrm{I}
, which is the image under Yoneda of the canonical coequalizer
[1]\rightrightarrows[0]\rightarrow(0)
in
\mathbb{B}_{=}
. The closed complement topology for
[0=1]\rightarrowtail 1
is generated by the single cover
0\rightarrow[0=1]
, which can be described logically as
forcing the sequent
(0=1\vdash\bot)
to hold. Recall from
[
Joh77
]
, Proposition 3.53, the following simple characterization of the sheaves for the closed complement topology of an object
U\rightarrowtail 1
: an object
X
is a sheaf iff
X\times U\cong U
. In the present case, it therefore suffices to show that for any
P:\mathbb{B}_{=}\rightarrow\mathsf{Set}
we have:

P\times[0=1]\cong[0=1]\quad\text{iff}\quad P(n)=1\ \text{for all $n$}.

For any object
b\in\mathbb{B}_{=}
, consider the map

\mathrm{Hom}(yb,P\times[0=1])\cong\mathrm{Hom}(yb,P)\times\mathrm{Hom}(yb,[0=1])\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(yb,[0=1]).

If
b=[k]
, then
\mathrm{Hom}(yb,[0=1])\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{B}_{=}}((0),[k])\cong 0
, and so we always have an iso

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}(yb,P\times[0=1])\cong\mathrm{Hom}(yb,P)\times\mathrm{Hom}(yb,[0=1])\\
\cong\mathrm{Hom}(yb,P)\times 0\cong 0.\end{split}

If
b=(k)
, then
\mathrm{Hom}(y(k),[0=1])\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{B}_{=}}((0),(k))\cong 1
, and we have an iso

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}(y(k),P\times[\bot=\top])\cong\mathrm{Hom}(y(k),P)\times\mathrm{Hom}(y(k),[0=1])\\
\cong\mathrm{Hom}(y(k),P)\times 1\cong\mathrm{Hom}(y(k),P)\cong P(k).\end{split}

Thus either way we will have an iso
P\times[0=1]\cong[0=1]
iff
P(k)\cong 1
.

The presheaf topos
\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}}
is therefore the closed complement of the open subtopos

\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}_{=}}\!/_{\![0=1]}\ \hookrightarrow\ \mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}_{=}}\,,

given by forcing the proposition
0\neq 1
. Since
\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}_{=}}
is clearly the classifying topos for
arbitrary
bipointed objects, say
\mathsf{Set}[B,0,1]
, the sheaf subtopos

\mathsf{Set}[B,0\neq 1]\ \simeq\ \mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}}\ \hookrightarrow\ \mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{B}_{=}}\ \simeq\ \mathsf{Set}[B,0,1]

classifies
strictly
bipointed objects,
i.e.
intervals, as claimed.
∎

Corollary 129
.

The geometric realization functor to topological spaces

R:\mathsf{cSet}\rightarrow\mathsf{Top}

preserves finite products,
R(X\times Y)\cong R(X)\times R(Y)
and
R(1)\cong\{*\}
.

Proof.

Compose the inverse image of the classifying geometric morphism
\mathsf{sSets}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}
of the
1
-simplex
\Delta^{1}
with the standard geometric realization
\mathsf{sSets}\rightarrow\mathsf{Top}
, both of which preserve finite products.
∎

Example 130

(P. Aczel)
.

The cubical set
P
of polynomials (say, over the integers), is defined by:

P_{n}=\{p(x_{1},...,x_{n})\ |\ \text{polynomials in at most}\ x_{1},...,x_{n}\}

with the substitution map
s^{*}:P_{n}\rightarrow P_{m}
taking
p(x_{1},...,x_{n})
to

s^{*}p(x_{1},...,x_{n})=p\big(s(x_{1}),\dots,s(x_{n})\big)\,,

for each bipointed map
s:[n]\rightarrow[m]
.

This cubical set
P
underlies a ring object in
\mathsf{cSet}
, and the interval
\mathrm{I}=\mathsf{y}[1]
embeds into it via the component maps

\eta_{n}:\mathrm{I}_{n}\rightarrow P_{n}

taking
v_{i}\in\boxempty([n],[1])\cong\mathbb{B}\big([1],[n]\big)\cong\{0,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},1\}
to
0
,
1
, or the variable
x_{i}
, respectively, in
P_{n}
.
The same is true for any algebraic theory
\mathbb{T}
with two constants,
such as Boolean algebras: there is a distinguished cubical
\mathbb{T}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, and a natural map
\eta:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow|\mathcal{A}|
in
\mathsf{cSet}
.

Indeed, let
\mathsf{cSet}=\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{I}]
as a classifying topos for intervals by Proposition
128
with
\mathcal{I}=(1\rightrightarrows\mathrm{I})
, and let

\mathsf{Set}[\mathbb{T},\mathsf{flat}]=\mathsf{Set}^{{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{op}}}

be the topos of presheaves on the Lawvere algebraic theory
\mathbb{T}
, which therefore classifies
flat

\mathbb{T}
-algebras. There is a bipointed object
\mathcal{J}=(1\rightrightarrows\mathrm{J})
in
\mathbb{T}
, consisting of the generic
\mathbb{T}
-algebra and its two constants, which has a classifying functor
\mathrm{J}^{\sharp}:\boxempty\rightarrow\mathbb{T}
, inducing adjoint functors on presheaves,

\mathrm{J}_{!}\dashv\mathrm{J}^{*}\dashv\mathrm{J}_{*}:\mathsf{Set}[\mathcal{I}]=\mathsf{Set}^{\boxempty^{\mathrm{op}}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Set}^{\mathbb{T}^{\mathrm{op}}}=\mathsf{Set}[\mathbb{T},\mathsf{flat}]\,,

where
\mathrm{J}_{!}\circ\mathsf{y}_{\boxempty}=\ \mathsf{y}_{\mathbb{T}}\circ\mathrm{J}^{\sharp}
, with
\mathsf{y}
the respective Yoneda embeddings.

We can then calculate,

\begin{split}\mathrm{J}^{*}\mathrm{J}_{!}(\mathrm{I})([n])&=\mathrm{J}^{*}\mathrm{J}_{!}(\mathsf{y}[1])([n])\\
&=\mathrm{J}^{*}\mathsf{y}(\mathrm{J}^{\sharp}[1])([n])=\mathsf{y}(\mathrm{J}^{\sharp}[1])(\mathrm{J}^{\sharp}[n])\\
&=\mathbb{T}(\mathrm{J}^{\sharp}[n],\mathrm{J}^{\sharp}[1])=\mathbb{T}\mathsf{Alg}(\mathrm{J}^{\sharp}[1],\mathrm{J}^{\sharp}[n])\\
&=\mathbb{T}\mathsf{Alg}(F(1),F(n))=|F(n)|,\end{split}

(172)

where
|F(n)|
is the underlying set of the free
\mathbb{T}
-algebra
F(n)
, the
n^{th}
object of the Lawvere theory under its dual presentation
{\mathbb{T}}^{\mathrm{op}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{T}\mathsf{Alg}
. The unit of the
J_{!}\dashv J^{*}
adjunction provides a natural map
\eta:\mathrm{I}\rightarrow\mathrm{J}^{*}\mathrm{J}_{!}(\mathrm{I})
, given pointwise by
\mathrm{I}_{n}\cong\{0,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},1\}\longrightarrow|F(n)|\cong\mathrm{J}^{*}\mathrm{J}_{!}(\mathrm{I})_{n}
.

The cubical set of polynomials
P=\mathrm{J}^{*}\mathrm{J}_{!}(\mathrm{I})
is thus indeed a cubical ring, with a map
\mathrm{I}\rightarrow P
, since
\mathrm{J}_{!}(\mathrm{I})\cong\mathsf{y}_{\mathbb{T}}\,\mathrm{J}^{\sharp}([1])\cong\mathsf{y}_{\mathbb{T}}(\mathrm{J})
is a ring in
\mathsf{Set}[\mathbb{T},\mathsf{flat}]
and
J^{*}
is left exact. In fact, we learn thereby that
P
is flat.

Definition 131
.

Let
\Box\rightarrow\mathsf{Cat}
be the unique product-preserving functor taking the interval
[1]
to the one arrow category
\mathbbm{2}=(0\leq 1)
. This functor then takes
[n]
to
\mathbbm{2}^{n}
, the
n
-fold product in
\mathsf{Cat}
, and maps
[m]\rightarrow[n]
to the corresponding monotone functions
\mathbbm{2}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbbm{2}^{n}
of posets.
3
3

3

Thus factoring through the full subcategory
\boxempty_{\wedge,\vee}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Cat}
of
Dedekind cubes
, mentioned above, which is the Lawvere algebraic theory of distributive lattices.
The
cubical nerve
functor

N:\mathsf{Cat}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}

is then defined by:

N(\mathbb{C})_{n}=\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{n},\mathbb{C}).

Thus
N(\mathbb{C})_{0}
is the set of objects of
\mathbb{C}
;
N(\mathbb{C})_{1}
is the set of arrows;
N(\mathbb{C})_{2}
consists of all commutative squares;
N(\mathbb{C})_{3}
all commutative cubes, etc.

Proposition 132
.

The cubical nerve
N:\mathsf{Cat}\rightarrow\mathsf{cSet}
is full and faithful.

Proof.

Given categories
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
and functors
F,G:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}
, suppose
F(f)\neq G(f)
for some
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathbb{C}
. Take
f^{\sharp}:\mathbbm{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
with image
f
. Then
N(F)_{1}(f^{\sharp})=F(f)\neq G(f)=N(G)_{1}(f^{\sharp})
, and so
N(F)\neq N(G):N(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow N(\mathbb{D})
. So
N
is faithful.

For fullness, let
\varphi:N(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow N(\mathbb{D})
be a natural transformation, and define a proposed functor
F:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}
by

\displaystyle F_{0}=\varphi_{0}

\displaystyle:\mathbb{C}_{0}=N(\mathbb{C})_{0}\rightarrow N(\mathbb{D})_{0}=\mathbb{D}_{0}

\displaystyle F_{1}=\varphi_{1}

\displaystyle:\mathbb{C}_{1}=N(\mathbb{C})_{1}\rightarrow N(\mathbb{D})_{1}=\mathbb{D}_{1}.

We just need to show that
F
preserves identity arrows and composition.
Consider the following diagram.

\textstyle{\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{1},\mathbb{C})=N(\mathbb{C})_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}}
\textstyle{N(\mathbb{D})_{1}=\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{1},\mathbb{D})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{0},\mathbb{C})=N(\mathbb{C})_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!^{*}}
\scriptstyle{F_{0}}
\textstyle{N(\mathbb{D})_{0}=\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{0},\mathbb{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{!^{*}}

Here
!^{*}:\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{0},\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2},\mathbb{C})
is precomposition with
!:\mathbbm{2}=\mathbbm{2}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbbm{2}^{0}=\mathbbm{1}
, so the diagram commutes. But since
!:\mathbbm{2}\rightarrow\mathbbm{1}
is a functor,

\mathbb{C}_{0}=\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{1},\mathbb{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!^{*}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2},\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{C}_{1}

takes objects in
\mathbb{C}
to their identity arrows. Thus
F
preserves identity arrows. Similarly, for composition, consider

\textstyle{\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{2},\mathbb{C})=N(\mathbb{C})_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{2}}
\textstyle{N(\mathbb{D})_{2}=\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{2},\mathbb{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2},\mathbb{C})=N(\mathbb{C})_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}}
\textstyle{N(\mathbb{D})_{1}=\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2},\mathbb{D}).}

where
\varphi_{2}:N(\mathbb{C})_{2}\rightarrow N(\mathbb{D})_{2}
is the action of
\varphi
on commutative squares of arrows, and
d^{*}:\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{2},\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2},\mathbb{C})
is precomposition with the diagonal map
d:\mathbbm{2}\rightarrow\mathbbm{2}^{2}=\mathbbm{2}\times\mathbbm{2}
, so the diagram commutes. For any composable
f:A\rightarrow B
and
g:B\rightarrow C
in
\mathbb{C}
there is a commutative square

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{C,}

and the effect of
d^{*}:\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{2},\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2},\mathbb{C})
on this square is exactly
g\circ f:A\rightarrow C
, and similarly for
d^{*}:\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2}^{2},\mathbb{D})\rightarrow\mathsf{Cat}(\mathbbm{2},\mathbb{D})
. Thus the commutativity of the above diagram implies that
F
preserves composition.
Since clearly
N(F)=\varphi
, we indeed have that
N
is also full.
∎
